					<description>Your teams get an AI assistant that handles real work while your data stays in your environment and your conversations stay private Today, the Amazon Quick desktop application is generally available on macOS and Windows. We’re also adding a new activity feed to the mobile experience on iOS and Android that consolidates email, calendar, CRM, […]</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Your teams get an AI assistant that handles real work while your data stays in your environment and your conversations stay private&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Today, the Amazon Quick desktop application is generally available on macOS and Windows. We’re also adding a new activity feed to the mobile experience on iOS and Android that consolidates email, calendar, CRM, and messaging into one prioritized view, showing your teams the decisions that need them while agents resolve the routine items in the background, so they spend less time sorting through updates and more time on high-value work.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Enterprise IT organizations face a compounding problem: the tools your teams need to stay productive are multiplying, and so is the shadow AI risk that comes with them. When people work around approved systems to get things done faster, governance breaks down, data leaves controlled environments, and IT loses visibility. The answer is not to restrict AI access but to give teams an enterprise-grade AI assistant built for work, on infrastructure IT already controls. Quick runs on AWS, the same infrastructure behind the most security-sensitive workloads on the planet. Your data stays in your environment, your conversations remain private, and everything is auditable. Full audit trails are available through Amazon CloudWatch and AWS CloudTrail. The compliance certifications your security team requires (HIPAA, FedRAMP, SOC 2, and ISO 27001) are built in from day one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Customers across manufacturing, healthcare, and sports have been using Quick on desktop during preview—asking complex questions in natural language and getting grounded, trustworthy answers in seconds, cutting time-to-insight significantly while keeping governance and accuracy built in from the start.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For organizations managing knowledge workers across hundreds of roles and workflows, the capacity equation matters too. Business goals keep growing, but the hours in a day do not. Most of the time that should go to high-judgment work gets consumed by pulling information together, chasing follow-ups, and assembling deliverables. Quick changes that equation by providing a thought partner that knows your context, builds what you need, and takes action on your behalf. Quick also gives your team a shared workspace where the dashboards, agents, and automations one person builds are available to the whole team.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Here is what Quick looks like across a real workday, from delegating meeting prep on a laptop to triaging priorities on a phone and following up after the workday ends.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Here, an enterprise account executive at a manufacturing company uses Quick to dramatically simplify prep for a key customer meeting—typing a single request and letting Quick assemble the brief in the background&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;iframe loading="lazy" title="Amazon Quick: Prep for Any Meeting in One Prompt | Amazon Web Services" width="500" height="281" src="https://www.youtube-nocookie.com/embed/K79igsRD59s?feature=oembed" frameborder="0" allow="accelerometer; autoplay; clipboard-write; encrypted-media; gyroscope; picture-in-picture; web-share" referrerpolicy="strict-origin-when-cross-origin" allowfullscreen sandbox="allow-scripts allow-same-origin"&gt;&lt;/iframe&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Focus on what matters, not what’s loudest&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;span data-contrast="auto"&gt;Most work tools generate a firehose of notifications.&amp;nbsp;Quick’s&amp;nbsp;activity feed does the opposite: it&amp;nbsp;consolidates&amp;nbsp;signals from email, messaging, CRM, and calendar into one prioritized view.&amp;nbsp;Items&amp;nbsp;agents resolve on their own disappear. What&amp;nbsp;remains&amp;nbsp;is a short queue of things only you can act on, and the feed gets smarter over time. Quick&amp;nbsp;learns&amp;nbsp;your relationships, priorities, and patterns, so it surfaces what matters most, not just what arrived most recently.&lt;/span&gt;&lt;span data-ccp-props="{&amp;quot;201341983&amp;quot;:0,&amp;quot;335559739&amp;quot;:200,&amp;quot;335559740&amp;quot;:276}"&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;i&gt;&lt;span data-contrast="auto"&gt;Here, the same rep triages her morning from her phone,&amp;nbsp;approving an addition to her meeting brief and responding to a pipeline review request for her VP, without opening another app.&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/i&gt;&lt;span data-ccp-props="{&amp;quot;201341983&amp;quot;:0,&amp;quot;335559738&amp;quot;:120,&amp;quot;335559740&amp;quot;:276}"&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;iframe loading="lazy" title="Amazon Quick: Triage Your Morning from Your Phone | Amazon Web Services" width="500" height="281" src="https://www.youtube-nocookie.com/embed/H8R5sOuCSIo?feature=oembed" frameborder="0" allow="accelerometer; autoplay; clipboard-write; encrypted-media; gyroscope; picture-in-picture; web-share" referrerpolicy="strict-origin-when-cross-origin" allowfullscreen sandbox="allow-scripts allow-same-origin"&gt;&lt;/iframe&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;span data-contrast="none"&gt;For teams&amp;nbsp;operating&amp;nbsp;at enterprise scale, that prioritization is what turns a firehose of updates into a clear path forward:&lt;/span&gt;&lt;span data-ccp-props="{&amp;quot;201341983&amp;quot;:0,&amp;quot;335559738&amp;quot;:200,&amp;quot;335559739&amp;quot;:80,&amp;quot;335559740&amp;quot;:276}"&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;i&gt;&lt;span data-contrast="none"&gt;“At Southwest Airlines,&amp;nbsp;we’re&amp;nbsp;building agentic AI tools and autonomous agents to streamline operations across our 70,000+ Employees and enhance the experience for the millions of Customers we serve every day. Amazon Quick Desktop gives our teams the ability to ask complex questions in natural language and get grounded, trustworthy answers in seconds instead of waiting on ad hoc report requests. Our developers build once and deploy intelligent experiences across the enterprise, with governance and accuracy baked in from the start. Quick cuts time-to-insight significantly and is already powering high-impact use cases across the&amp;nbsp;organization&amp;nbsp;including market analytics.&amp;nbsp;It’s&amp;nbsp;become the foundation of how we put AI-driven intelligence directly into the hands of our People.”&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/i&gt;&lt;span data-ccp-props="{&amp;quot;134233117&amp;quot;:false,&amp;quot;134233118&amp;quot;:false,&amp;quot;335551550&amp;quot;:0,&amp;quot;335551620&amp;quot;:0,&amp;quot;335557856&amp;quot;:16777215,&amp;quot;335559685&amp;quot;:360,&amp;quot;335559737&amp;quot;:360,&amp;quot;335559738&amp;quot;:160,&amp;quot;335559739&amp;quot;:40}"&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;&lt;strong&gt;From meeting to momentum&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;span data-contrast="auto"&gt;Most AI tools answer questions. Quick completes tasks by synthesizing information across systems, drafting deliverables, updating records, and executing follow-ups on behalf of your team. Teams do not get a summary of what they&amp;nbsp;could&amp;nbsp;do. They get finished output, ready for review.&lt;/span&gt;&lt;span data-ccp-props="{&amp;quot;201341983&amp;quot;:0,&amp;quot;335559739&amp;quot;:200,&amp;quot;335559740&amp;quot;:276}"&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;i&gt;&lt;span data-contrast="auto"&gt;Here, she&amp;nbsp;picks up on&amp;nbsp;her laptop where she left off on&amp;nbsp;mobile—reviewing&amp;nbsp;the completed work in mission control and sharing the finished brief with her account team.&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/i&gt;&lt;span data-ccp-props="{&amp;quot;201341983&amp;quot;:0,&amp;quot;335559738&amp;quot;:120,&amp;quot;335559740&amp;quot;:276}"&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;iframe loading="lazy" title="Amazon Quick: Pick Up Right Where You Left Off | Amazon Web Services" width="500" height="281" src="https://www.youtube-nocookie.com/embed/CAuKlT3nkNY?feature=oembed" frameborder="0" allow="accelerometer; autoplay; clipboard-write; encrypted-media; gyroscope; picture-in-picture; web-share" referrerpolicy="strict-origin-when-cross-origin" allowfullscreen sandbox="allow-scripts allow-same-origin"&gt;&lt;/iframe&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;span data-contrast="auto"&gt;Early customers point to that shift&amp;nbsp;from answers to finished work&amp;nbsp;as the thing that changes how their teams&amp;nbsp;operate:&lt;/span&gt;&lt;span data-ccp-props="{&amp;quot;201341983&amp;quot;:0,&amp;quot;335559738&amp;quot;:200,&amp;quot;335559739&amp;quot;:200,&amp;quot;335559740&amp;quot;:276}"&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;h1&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The work follows through, even when you don’t have time&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;span data-contrast="auto"&gt;Quick is built on the tools, systems, and infrastructure your organization already runs on. There is no migration, no new ecosystem to adopt. It connects to what you have and starts delivering value on day one.&lt;/span&gt;&lt;span data-ccp-props="{&amp;quot;201341983&amp;quot;:0,&amp;quot;335559739&amp;quot;:200,&amp;quot;335559740&amp;quot;:276}"&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;p&gt;&lt;span data-contrast="auto"&gt;Every decision stays yours while Quick handles the rest.&lt;/span&gt;&lt;span data-ccp-props="{&amp;quot;201341983&amp;quot;:0,&amp;quot;335559738&amp;quot;:200,&amp;quot;335559739&amp;quot;:200,&amp;quot;335559740&amp;quot;:276}"&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

You bring the judgment; Quick gives your teams the capacity to match their goals.&lt;/span&gt;&lt;span data-ccp-props="{&amp;quot;201341983&amp;quot;:0,&amp;quot;335559739&amp;quot;:200,&amp;quot;335559740&amp;quot;:276}"&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Build an end-to-end RFI questionnaire workflow using Amazon Quick Automate</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-an-end-to-end-rfi-questionnaire-workflow-using-amazon-quick-automate/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Chaytanya Kumar]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 10 Sep 2026 16:08:57 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Quick Suite]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Simple Storage Service (S3)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">c7589b0f40b52eb8334f11f9000d87654f7f0e92</guid>

					<description>Learn how to build an end-to-end RFI questionnaire workflow with Amazon Quick Automate. Read a multi-tab RFI workbook from Amazon S3, use natural-language prompts to extract and structure the questionnaire data, refine the workflow through conversation, and write clean CSV output back to Amazon S3 — cutting development from days to hours.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Build an end-to-end Request for Information (RFI) questionnaire workflow using Amazon Quick Automate to solve a challenge organizations face at every scale. A typical enterprise might handle hundreds of RFI questionnaires each year, and each arrives as a complex, multi-tab workbook packed with hierarchical question sets, category metadata, and varied response types. The volume, variety, and complexity of these documents create a significant operational challenge. Manually extracting, structuring, and resolving RFI questions requires repeated coordination, delays responses, and can introduce errors each time the questionnaire format changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quicksuite/automate/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick Automate&lt;/a&gt; handles multi-agent automation for supported enterprise processes that can span across departments, systems, UI and API interactions, and third-party systems. It uses a team of agents to simplify business process management and cut down on maintenance overhead. You describe your processing goals in plain language, and Quick Automate can produce an executable workflow covering ingestion, transformation, validation, and output. You can refine the workflow through conversation, run it against your data, and promote validated versions across AWS Regions. This approach can help you respond to RFIs faster and reduce formatting inconsistencies and errors. The automation is designed to scale with your workload, and you can adapt it to new questionnaire formats by updating the natural-language instructions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we show you how to build an end-to-end automation that reads a multi-tab RFI workbook from &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)&lt;/a&gt;, extracts and structures the questionnaire data, and writes clean output back to Amazon S3. This tutorial uses Amazon Quick Automate and Amazon S3. Review &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick pricing&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon S3 pricing&lt;/a&gt; for cost details.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This walkthrough automates the processing of an RFI workbook stored in Amazon S3. When procurement teams receive an RFI questionnaire, they need to extract each question and preserve its category and response type. The goal is to produce a structured dataset that downstream teams can consume, interpret, and act on. In this example we present a solution that automates the process using Amazon Quick Automate. You start by connecting Amazon Quick Automate to the Amazon S3 bucket where your RFI workbooks are stored. Then you create an automation project, which is where you select your data connectors and organize your workflows. With the project in place, you can describe your processing logic in plain language: what to read, how to structure it, and where to write the output. The result is a working automation that reads a multi-tab RFI workbook, extracts and structures the questionnaire data, and outputs a clean comma-separated values (CSV) file. It does this without requiring custom code for common scenarios. The following diagram shows the high-level sequence of steps.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 394px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/01/ML-20411-1.png" alt="Flow diagram of the RFI workbook automation from connecting the data source to deploying the finished automation in production" width="384"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: High-level sequence of steps for the RFI workbook automation&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The diagram takes you from connecting your data source through deploying the finished automation in a production environment. Check the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Regions&lt;/a&gt; page for the latest availability.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The solution follows these steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Set up an Amazon S3 action connector&lt;/strong&gt; – Connect Amazon Quick Automate to your S3 bucket.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Add the S3 integration to an automation group&lt;/strong&gt; – Share the S3 action with the automation group that will use it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Create an automation project&lt;/strong&gt; – Create the project where you describe your processing logic.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Describe the processing logic&lt;/strong&gt; – Enter a natural-language prompt and let the generative AI assistant build the workflow.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Refine through conversation&lt;/strong&gt; – Iterate on the generated workflow by requesting targeted changes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Validate results&lt;/strong&gt; – Run the workflow and verify output in your pre-production (development) AWS account.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Promote with Import/Export&lt;/strong&gt; – Export the validated version and import it into your production account or target AWS Region.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Now that we have reviewed the high-level steps for the solution, we will go over the environment requirements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you begin, confirm that your environment meets the following requirements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An Amazon Quick Enterprise subscription with Amazon Quick Automate access.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An Amazon S3 bucket in the same AWS Region as the Amazon Quick application.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Familiarity with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)&lt;/a&gt; roles and policies.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Basic understanding of &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon S3&lt;/a&gt; buckets, prefixes, and objects.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="iam-baseline-for-this-walkthrough"&gt;IAM baseline for this walkthrough&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You need an IAM role that grants Amazon Quick Automate permission to access your S3 bucket. If you don’t already have one, follow these instructions to create it now:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;IAM console&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the navigation pane, choose &lt;strong&gt;Roles&lt;/strong&gt;, then &lt;strong&gt;Create role&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For &lt;strong&gt;Trusted entity type&lt;/strong&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;Custom trust policy&lt;/strong&gt; and paste the following trust policy:&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Principal": {
        "Service": "quicksight.amazonaws.com"
      },
      "Action": "sts:AssumeRole"
    }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="4" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Next&lt;/strong&gt; and attach a permissions policy that grants s3:GetObject, s3:PutObject, and s3:ListBucket on your target bucket.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Name the role (for example, QuickAutomate-S3-Role) and choose &lt;strong&gt;Create role&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Copy the role Amazon Resource Name (ARN). You will need it when configuring the S3 connector.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the IAM role created and the role ARN copied, you’re ready to configure the connection between Amazon Quick Automate and your S3 bucket.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="set-up-the-s3-connector"&gt;Set up the S3 connector&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After prerequisites are confirmed, configure the S3 connector in the Amazon Quick console.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the Amazon Quick console, choose the user icon in the upper-right corner.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open &lt;strong&gt;Manage Account&lt;/strong&gt;, then go to &lt;strong&gt;Permissions&lt;/strong&gt; &amp;gt; &lt;strong&gt;AWS Actions&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/01/ML-20411-2.png" alt="AWS Actions page under Permissions in the Amazon Quick console Manage Account settings" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: AWS Actions page in the Amazon Quick console&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="3" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;New action&lt;/strong&gt;, then select &lt;strong&gt;Amazon S3&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Configure the connector:&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enter a connector name (for example, ExampleBucket-S3-Connection) and a description.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Paste the ARN of the IAM role you created in the prerequisites step.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/01/ML-20411-3.png" alt="Amazon S3 action connector configuration form with connector name, description, and IAM role ARN fields" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Amazon S3 action connector configuration&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="5" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The console validates the role trust policy automatically. Confirm that the status shows a successful connection.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Share the connector with users and groups who build and run workflows.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="create-an-automation-group"&gt;Create an automation group&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the Amazon Quick console, go to &lt;strong&gt;Automations&lt;/strong&gt; &amp;gt; &lt;strong&gt;Create Group&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/01/ML-20411-4.png" alt="Automations page in the Amazon Quick console showing the Create Group option" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Create Group option on the Automations page&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="2" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Name the group (for example, RFI-Processing-Group).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;On the &lt;strong&gt;Add actions&lt;/strong&gt; page, select the Amazon S3 connection you created earlier, then choose &lt;strong&gt;Next&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 752px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/01/ML-20411-5.png" alt="Add actions page with the Amazon S3 connection selected for the automation group" width="742"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Selecting Amazon S3 for the automation group&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the connector and the automation group configured, create the automation project that will hold your workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="create-an-automation-project"&gt;Create an automation project&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The automation project is where you describe your processing logic and where the generated workflow lives.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;On the &lt;strong&gt;Automations&lt;/strong&gt; page, choose the &lt;strong&gt;Projects&lt;/strong&gt; tab, then choose &lt;strong&gt;Create Project&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In &lt;strong&gt;Provide project details&lt;/strong&gt;, enter a name for the project, select the automation group you created, and optionally add a description and upload existing documentation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Next: Business case&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The business case section is optional and is used for tracking return on investment. To complete it, enter hours saved per case, cases per year, project priority, and a target launch date.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Create&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Your project opens on a summary page with &lt;strong&gt;Summary&lt;/strong&gt;, &lt;strong&gt;Versions&lt;/strong&gt;, and &lt;strong&gt;Deployments&lt;/strong&gt; tabs, and a status panel showing the current state of the project.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the connector, the automation group, and the project in place, you are ready to describe your workflow logic.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="describe-the-processing-logic-in-natural-language"&gt;Describe the processing logic in natural language&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In Amazon Quick Automate, you describe what the automation should do using natural language. You can type directly into the prompt area or upload a file with your requirements. The AI assistant interprets your instructions and generates executable workflow steps.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-connect-to-s3-and-read-a-sheet"&gt;Step 1: Connect to S3 and read a sheet&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the automation project, you see a text area where you describe your processing requirements. Enter a prompt that covers the data source, transformation logic, and the output you want. For example:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Connect to my S3 bucket using the ExampleBucket-S3-Connection connector. Read the file SampleDepartment_Version2.xlsx from the sample-folder prefix. Open the Strategy sheet and extract all survey questions. Identify main questions and subquestions based on numbering and indentation. Structure the data with columns for Serial Number, Category, Question, and Response Type. Write the output as a CSV file to the output-folder prefix in the same bucket.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/01/ML-20411-6.png" alt="Automation project prompt area with the natural-language processing instructions entered" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: Entering the processing prompt in the automation project&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-review-the-generated-workflow"&gt;Step 2: Review the generated workflow&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After you submit the prompt, the AI assistant generates a multi-step workflow. Each step maps to a part of your instructions:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Read data&lt;/strong&gt; – Connects to S3, downloads the workbook, and reads the target sheet with headers.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Extract and transform&lt;/strong&gt; – Identifies main questions and subquestions by numbering and indentation, and pulls category metadata from column headers.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Structure output&lt;/strong&gt; – Converts subquestions into standalone records by merging parent context. Defines the output schema.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Write results&lt;/strong&gt; – Saves structured output as a CSV file to the specified S3 location.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 748px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image7.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/01/ML-20411-7.png" alt="Generated multi-step workflow showing read, extract and transform, structure output, and write steps" width="738"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: Generated multi-step workflow&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because the AI assistant relies on generative AI, the exact steps and wording it produces might vary between runs. The workflow you see might differ slightly from this example, but it covers the same logical operations based on your instructions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-run-and-validate"&gt;Step 3: Run and validate&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Run&lt;/strong&gt; to execute the workflow against your workbook. Review the output to confirm:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;All questions are extracted (main and subquestions).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Category text matches the source column headers exactly.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Subquestions include parent context.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Response types are preserved as-is from the source.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image8.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/01/ML-20411-8.png" alt="Workflow run output listing extracted questions with serial number, category, question, and response type columns" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 8: Workflow run output&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="refine-the-workflow-through-conversation"&gt;Refine the workflow through conversation&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When the output needs improvement, use the conversation interface to request targeted changes. Reference the specific step and describe the expected behavior.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Example refinement prompts:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;em&gt;“The step that reads the Strategy sheet should also capture column headers as metadata.”&lt;/em&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;em&gt;“The category field does not match source headers. Update extraction to use exact header text.”&lt;/em&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;em&gt;“In the subquestion transformation step, merge parent context into the question text.”&lt;/em&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Follow this iterative cycle:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Identify the issue&lt;/strong&gt; – Compare output against your expected results.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Request a targeted update&lt;/strong&gt; – Describe the change in natural language, referencing the specific step.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Review the updated workflow&lt;/strong&gt; – The AI assistant modifies the relevant step and shows you the change.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Re-run and validate&lt;/strong&gt; – Execute again and confirm the fix.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="best-practices"&gt;Best practices&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before promoting your automation to production, follow these best practices:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Validate incrementally&lt;/strong&gt; – Run and check output after each refinement, rather than batching multiple changes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Handle edge cases explicitly&lt;/strong&gt; – Describe how the workflow should behave when data is missing, duplicated, or formatted inconsistently.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="promote-automations-with-importexport"&gt;Promote automations with Import/Export&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Quick Automate Import/Export supports controlled promotion from pre-production to production and consistent reuse across AWS Regions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="export-a-version"&gt;Export a version&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open the source automation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Go to &lt;strong&gt;Versions&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Export version&lt;/strong&gt; for the validated release.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image9.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/01/ML-20411-9.png" alt="Versions tab of an automation with the Export version option for a validated release" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 9: Exporting a validated automation version&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="4" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Copy the generated version link.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; The export link expires after a set period. Copy and use it promptly, or generate a new link if it has expired.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image10.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/01/ML-20411-10.png" alt="Generated export version link shown after exporting the automation version" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 10: Generated export version link&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="import-a-version"&gt;Import a version&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open or create an Amazon Quick Automate project in the destination account or AWS Region.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Import version&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Paste the version link and choose &lt;strong&gt;Start&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Review version details and complete the import.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 762px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image11.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/01/ML-20411-11.png" alt="Import version dialog where the exported version link is pasted into the destination project" width="752"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 11: Importing an automation version&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="5" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Configure required connectors, credentials, and deployment settings in the target environment.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 790px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image12.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/01/ML-20411-12.png" alt="Target environment configuration screen for connectors and deployment settings after import" width="780"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 12: Configuring the target environment after import&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the automation project and the automation group.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Remove the S3 connector if no other workflows use it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete test IAM roles and test S3 objects when no longer required.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For detailed cleanup guidance, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/using-amazon-quick-automate.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Using Amazon Quick Automate&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you built an end-to-end automation for processing RFI questionnaires stored as multi-tab Excel workbooks using Amazon Quick Automate. You connected your data through an Amazon S3 action connector, described your processing logic in natural language, refined the automation through conversation, and promoted it across AWS accounts and Regions using Import/Export.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can extend this approach to other business workflows, such as:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Intelligent document processing (IDP)&lt;/strong&gt; – Extract structured data from invoices, contracts, or forms.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Report consolidation&lt;/strong&gt; – Merge multi-sheet financial reports into a unified dataset.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;UI automation&lt;/strong&gt; – Automate repetitive browser-based tasks that feed into downstream analytics.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Survey data aggregation&lt;/strong&gt; – Consolidate survey responses from multiple sources into a single output.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For more on automation capabilities, action connectors, and natural-language workflows, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick documentation&lt;/a&gt;. Visit &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/getting-started.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Getting started with Amazon Quick&lt;/a&gt; to start using Amazon Quick Automate today.&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Model-agnostic PII detection with LLMs</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/model-agnostic-pii-detection-with-llms/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Christophe Dupuy]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 10 Sep 2026 16:02:16 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">4c4240b0ba11b392a9635e42bce173f72181033c</guid>

					<description>A configurable, model-agnostic detector that turns any large language model on Amazon Bedrock into a PII detector. Because the entities to detect live in a prompt rather than in code, one detector adapts to new entity types without retraining, and it outperforms an off-the-shelf tool across five public corpora and nine LLM-based detectors.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;A configurable, instruction-driven detector that runs on any large language model (LLM) managed on Amazon Bedrock, evaluated on five public PII corpora across nine LLM-based detectors, including the OpenAI PrivacyFilter.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Fine-tuning a model on real-world text creates a personally identifiable information (PII) detection problem. Training corpora are full of PII: names, home addresses, email and phone numbers, national-ID and social-security numbers, bank accounts, dates of birth. A model trained on uncleaned text can memorize that data and later reproduce it, leaking a real person’s details through a prompt that was never meant to surface them. In this post, we describe a configurable, model-agnostic detector built on large language models (LLMs), walk through its implementation, benchmark it against an off-the-shelf tool, and show how to run it on your own data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Sample code:&lt;/strong&gt; The detector described in this post ships as the pii-detector package, available in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-llm-pii-detection" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sample-llm-pii-detection repository&lt;/a&gt;. Every code snippet that follows is drawn from that package, and the &lt;em&gt;Running the detector end to end&lt;/em&gt; section walks through installing and running it on your own data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;PII rarely sits in a tidy form field. It hides in customer-support transcripts, HR records, chat logs, and the long free-text columns that make up the custom datasets teams fine-tune on. It arrives in messy, multilingual formats that no fixed schema anticipated. The usual tools are bi-directional token-classification models: transformer taggers that label each token with a PII type fixed at training time. A domain-specific identifier like an employee ID or crypto-wallet address is exactly what a custom fine-tuning corpus introduces, and it falls outside that frozen schema. Adding it means relabeling and retraining. And they are locked to one model and one deployment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Large language models reframe the problem. An LLM reads its instructions at inference time, so the entities to detect, the output format, and the deployment backend all become configuration rather than code. One detector can target a new entity type by editing a prompt instead of retraining, run on a managed API or inside your own virtual private cloud (VPC), and reason about context across eight languages without a translation step. The rest of this post describes such a detector, walks through the engineering behind it, and shows how it measures up against existing tools.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The detector treats the language model as a configurable, swappable component. You wrap the input text in instructions that define the PII entities to detect and the expected output. The model then returns a structured list of detected entities. Two design choices make it model-agnostic:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Instruction-driven detection:&lt;/strong&gt; The detection logic lives entirely in the instructions and a thin parsing layer. That makes it independent of any single model’s idiosyncrasies.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Configurable backend:&lt;/strong&gt; The model is reached through a uniform inference interface, the Inferencer. The package ships an adapter for &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; (managed, for example Mistral or OSS-GPT). The same interface accepts a custom adapter for an open model, such as OSS-GPT 20B served on your own infrastructure with a GPU. That covers secure or air-gapped environments that cannot reach Amazon Bedrock. Any object that takes a list of messages and returns the assistant’s text satisfies the interface, so the detector is agnostic to which backend it holds.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Customization comes from two independent components. The first is the model, which sets accuracy, latency, and cost: you choose a frontier Amazon Bedrock model or a small open model on a single GPU. The second is the entity set, which defines what counts as PII. To extend it, you add a domain-specific identifier or drop one that you don’t need. Changing the entity set is a one-line edit to the instructions, with no retraining and no redeployment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The LLM’s job is narrow and well-defined. It reads the text, identifies all PII spans, and labels each with an entity type from the schema. It returns those spans as structured JSON, and a post-processing step computes exact character offsets and removes duplicates.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To place the approach in context, we evaluate it span-for-span alongside eight other LLM-based detectors, including the &lt;a href="https://github.com/openai/privacy-filter" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenAI PrivacyFilter&lt;/a&gt;. All are scored on a common ground truth.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="technical-implementation"&gt;Technical implementation&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The detector is built from four parts. A prompt defines the schema, a backend runs the model, a parsing-and-offset layer turns the response into located spans, and a thin call sequence ties them together. This section walks through each part in the order a request flows through the system, pointing to the module in the package repository that implements it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="pii-schema-and-detection-prompt"&gt;PII schema and detection prompt&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The schema lives in a single system-prompt template, the heart of the detector: fifteen entity categories each with a one-line definition, a &lt;em&gt;do-not-flag&lt;/em&gt; list, optional few-shot examples, and the input text. Because the schema is text, adding or removing a category is a one-line edit. The model is instructed to respond with a JSON list, one object per detected entity carrying the entity type and the exact text value found. It does not return character offsets, which an LLM cannot produce reliably. Those are recovered in post-processing:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;[{"pii_entity_type": "FULL_ADDRESSES", "pii_entity_value": "82 Oak Street"},
{"pii_entity_type": "CONTACT_INFO", "pii_entity_value": "bob@example.com"}]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The full prompt is in &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-llm-pii-detection/blob/mainline/pii_detector/templates.py" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;pii_detector/templates.py&lt;/a&gt;, and the end-to-end walkthrough that follows runs it as-is against a sample string.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="llm-backend-integration"&gt;LLM backend integration&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because detection lives in the prompt, the backend is a free choice. In our provided implementation, the detector talks to a small interface, the Inferencer: &lt;em&gt;messages in, text out&lt;/em&gt;. The same detector therefore runs against a managed model on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; or an open model you host yourself on Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2). The package ships the Amazon Bedrock adapter (&lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-llm-pii-detection/blob/mainline/pii_detector/bedrock_inferencer.py" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;pii_detector/bedrock_inferencer.py&lt;/a&gt;), a thin wrapper over the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/APIReference/API_runtime_Converse.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Converse API&lt;/a&gt;. The walkthrough that follows runs that path end to end.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="post-processing"&gt;Post-processing&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The model’s raw text becomes a clean list of located spans in three steps, all in &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-llm-pii-detection/blob/mainline/pii_detector/detector.py" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;pii_detector/detector.py&lt;/a&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;JSON parsing:&lt;/strong&gt; The text response is converted into a list of dictionaries, each item corresponding to a detected PII (or an empty list if the record contains none).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Offset calculation:&lt;/strong&gt; Because the model returns values and not positions, each value is located in the source text with a regular expression.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Hallucinated-label recovery:&lt;/strong&gt; LLMs routinely emit near-miss labels (DATE for DATES, EMAIL for CONTACT_INFO), so each emitted label is re-homed onto the prompt’s own vocabulary by morphology and a curated alias table. A label that no tier can map is marked UNK rather than force-fitted, so genuine hallucinations stay visible.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="running-the-detector-end-to-end"&gt;Running the detector end to end&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This section walks through running the detector on your own data, from prerequisites to cleanup. Every step uses the pii-detector package referenced at the top of this post.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To follow along, you must have the following prerequisites.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Python:&lt;/strong&gt; Python 3.11 or later.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS account with Amazon Bedrock model access:&lt;/strong&gt; An AWS account whose credentials can call the Amazon Bedrock Converse API. You also need &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-access.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;model access enabled&lt;/a&gt; in the Amazon Bedrock console for the model that you choose, for example a Mistral or OSS-GPT model. For model availability by AWS Region, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Supported models by AWS Region&lt;/a&gt; in Amazon Bedrock. The detector resolves credentials through the standard AWS credential chain, so set AWS_PROFILE (or an IAM role or SSO profile) and AWS_REGION in your environment.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Python dependency:&lt;/strong&gt; Boto3, the only runtime dependency, installed into a virtual environment (see step 1).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following steps assume you have cloned the pii-detector repository and are working from its root directory.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-1-install-the-package-and-its-dependency"&gt;Step 1: Install the package and its dependency&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create a virtual environment and install boto3. The package runs from the repository root, so set PYTHONPATH to make the pii_detector module resolve.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cd pii-detector
python -m venv .venv &amp;amp;&amp;amp; source .venv/bin/activate
pip install boto3
export PYTHONPATH=.  # so `import pii_detector` resolves from the repo root&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-2-configure-aws-credentials-for-amazon-bedrock"&gt;Step 2: Configure AWS credentials for Amazon Bedrock&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Point Boto3 at an account with Amazon Bedrock access and select the Region where you enabled model access.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;export AWS_PROFILE=my-bedrock-profile
export AWS_REGION=us-east-1&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you aren’t using a named profile, Boto3 also supports AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID / AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY, but we recommend an AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) role or SSO profile instead of long-lived static keys.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-3-run-the-bundled-example"&gt;Step 3: Run the bundled example&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The repository ships a runnable example (&lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-llm-pii-detection/blob/mainline/examples/detect.py" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;examples/detect.py&lt;/a&gt;) that detects PII in a sample string. Run it as a module from the repository root. It fails fast with actionable guidance if credentials or model access are missing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python -m examples.detect&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-4-call-the-detector-on-your-own-text"&gt;Step 4: Call the detector on your own text&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Construct an Amazon Bedrock inferencer with any Amazon Bedrock Converse model id, wrap it in a PiiDetector, and call the detector on a string. It returns the list of located spans, each with its exact character offsets, ready to feed a downstream redaction step. There are no servers to manage, because Amazon Bedrock is fully managed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from pii_detector import BedrockInferencer, PiiDetector

inferencer = BedrockInferencer(
    model_id="openai.gpt-oss-20b-1:0",
    region="us-east-1",
)
detector = PiiDetector(inferencer)

spans = detector("Email bob@example.com or call Jane at 555-0142.")
# -&amp;gt; [{'pii_entity_type': 'CONTACT_INFO', 'pii_entity_value': 'bob@example.com',
# 'start': 6, 'end': 21}, ...]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;model_id&lt;/code&gt; is any Amazon Bedrock Converse model id or inference-profile id, for example amazon.nova-lite-v1:0 or mistral.mistral-large-3-675b-instruct. Switching models is a one-line change. The detector and the call site stay identical.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-5-clean-up"&gt;Step 5: Clean up&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock is serverless, so there’s no infrastructure to tear down and you pay only for the tokens you use. To clean up, deactivate the virtual environment (deactivate) and, if you no longer need it, disable the model access you enabled in the Amazon Bedrock console. If you supply your own self-hosted backend instead of Amazon Bedrock, remember to shut down that host yourself, since the detector doesn’t manage backend infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="benchmarking"&gt;Benchmarking&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Evaluation uses five public PII corpora from Hugging Face, each carrying ground-truth spans, sampling roughly 10,000 rows per dataset. Together they cover 49,365 records and 222,114 ground-truth core spans across eight languages (de, en, es, fr, hi, it, nl, te). Their domains run from multilingual synthetic profiles to English HR and customer-service documents, which makes the aggregate a fair stress test.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;#&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt;

   &lt;td&gt;9,993&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;63,931&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;US/UK. 30+ raw entity categories&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;span style="color: #ffffff"&gt;.&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Total&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;49,365&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;222,114&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;span style="color: #ffffff"&gt;.&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;A predicted span is matched to ground truth by exact (start, end, label) overlap (IoU = 1.0) and scored with Precision, Recall, and F1.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Comparing detectors across these datasets is harder than it looks, because labels do not line up. Each detector and each dataset uses its own vocabulary: &lt;code&gt;PRIVATE_NAMES&lt;/code&gt; compared to &lt;code&gt;NAME&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;street_address&lt;/code&gt; compared to &lt;code&gt;street&lt;/code&gt;. To make the comparison fair, every raw label, from detector output and dataset ground truth alike, is mapped onto a single canonical taxonomy of twelve common entities. Each detector is then scored only on the intersection of the label scopes it and the dataset both declare. This way, no detector is penalized for a category it never claimed to support.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Canonical entity&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Covers&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;NAME&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Private and public person names&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ADDRESS&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Full and partial addresses, locations&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CONTACT_INFO&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Email and phone&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;DATE&lt;/td&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;URL&lt;/td&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt;

   &lt;td&gt;Usernames&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt;

   &lt;td&gt;Passwords, PINs, access keys&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ID_NUMBER&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Passports, licenses, customer/employee IDs&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;The canonical core entity taxonomy. These twelve types are common across the datasets and detectors, so they form the basis of the headline comparison. The package repository gives the exact raw-label-to-canonical mapping for each of the five datasets.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This taxonomy defines two reporting scopes. Core F1, the fair head-to-head number, covers the twelve common entity types. Extended-entity F1 covers the dataset-specific categories (occupation, company name, crypto-wallet addresses, and similar) that most off-the-shelf detectors have no notion of. We cover that scope under Customization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The headline metric is span-level Core F1. The following table reports it together with estimated per-detection latency across a representative selection of LLM-based detectors. It covers managed models on Amazon Bedrock and open models served on Amazon EC2, including the OpenAI PrivacyFilter. The open models are chosen both smaller and larger than OSS-GPT 20B so the range is visible. Amazon Bedrock is model-agnostic, so the right choice depends on your workload’s accuracy, latency, and cost needs rather than any single ranking. Results vary by model. &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-llm-pii-detection/blob/mainline/docs/benchmarks.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;docs/benchmarks.md&lt;/a&gt; gives the complete table with every model we tested.&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Span-level Core F1 (all five datasets, 49,365 records) and estimated per-detection latency, grouped by backend (Amazon Bedrock, Amazon EC2). In practice detection runs over many records with parallel worker threads. The per-detection figure is the total wall-clock time extrapolated back to a single record, so it is indicative rather than a strict single-call measurement.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On the same corpora, Core F1 ranges from 74.9 percent (Nova Lite 2) to 83.1 percent (Mistral Large 3), with PrivacyFilter at 80.7 percent. Mistral Large 3 and OSS-GPT 120B run on Amazon Bedrock, and OSS-GPT 20B (81.6 percent) runs on hardware you control. Latency is driven by the model, not its parameter count. OSS-GPT 20B runs in about 1.2 seconds on Amazon EC2, while the similarly sized Qwen3.6-27B takes about 12.8 seconds, because reasoning verbosity and architecture matter more than raw size. And the backend is a free choice, since OSS-GPT 20B scores within 0.3 points on Amazon EC2 (81.6 percent) and Amazon Bedrock (81.3 percent).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Accuracy holds across languages and high-stakes identifiers. On the ai4privacy_500k breakdown (see the package repository), OSS-GPT 20B stays in a tight 83–90 percent Core-F1 band across all eight languages, including non-Latin Hindi and Telugu. It also scores at or above the frontier models on the identifiers that matter most: SSN, financial, and ID numbers all above 95 percent. The shared weak spot is DATE, at about 50 percent, where span boundaries and formats are genuinely ambiguous.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="customization"&gt;Customization&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The accuracy results so far exercised the model lever, where you trade accuracy against latency and cost. The second lever is the set of entities to detect, defined entirely in the instructions. This is where the approach extends beyond fixed-scope tools, and the clearest evidence is on the rare, domain-specific entities.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each dataset annotates its own beyond-core categories. The nemotron and gretel corpora label occupation, job title, and company name. The isotonic corpus labels crypto-wallet (Bitcoin and Ethereum) addresses, vehicle identifiers, and user-agent strings. The &lt;code&gt;ai4privacy_500k&lt;/code&gt; corpus labels sex, gender, and organization. A detector running the base configuration has no notion of these categories and scores near zero on them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Recovering them requires no new model and no retraining, only an instruction change. We call this the &lt;em&gt;Ext&lt;/em&gt; (Extended) configuration. It adds each dataset’s extra category definitions and a few worked examples to the prompt. It also removes the &lt;em&gt;do-not-flag&lt;/em&gt; lines that would otherwise conflict with them, for instance dropping “business addresses” from the public list once company name becomes a target. The package repository lists the full extra-category definitions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The effect is large, and it holds across all models we tested, frontier and small alike. Extended-entity F1 jumps several-fold while core accuracy is unchanged or slightly better:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;#&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Detector&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Backend&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Extended-entity F1 (base ▸ Ext)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Core F1 (base ▸ Ext)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Qwen3.6-35B-A3B&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;EC2&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;9.4% ▸ 80.5%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;79.4% ▸ 83.5%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;OSS-GPT 20B&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;EC2&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;12.1% ▸ 73.3%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;81.6% ▸ 83.1%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Mistral Large 3&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Bedrock&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;17.3% ▸ 72.7%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;83.1% ▸ 89.1%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;4&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Gemma-4-E4B-it&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;EC2&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;12.5% ▸ 72.5%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;79.4% ▸ 83.8%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Base prompt compared to the Extended (Ext) configuration, on the five public datasets, sorted by Extended-entity F1. Adding the extra category definitions lifts extended-entity F1 roughly six-fold and also nudges core F1 up. This works on all tested models, whereas a fixed-scope tagger cannot target these categories without retraining.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The same lever generalizes to brand-new entity types. To target one for a specific domain, add its definition and an example to the prompt. There is no model to fine-tune and no pipeline to redeploy. Combined with the freedom to choose the model behind it, this lets one detector adapt to each domain’s vocabulary at the instruction level.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An LLM-based PII detector turns the hardest constraints of off-the-shelf tools into configuration set by two levers. Those constraints are a fixed entity scope and lock-in to one model and one deployment. The model lever sets accuracy, latency, and cost. On five public corpora spanning eight languages, Core F1 across the nine detectors ranges from 74.9–83.1 percent, with PrivacyFilter at 80.7 percent. The open OSS-GPT 20B (81.6 percent) runs equally well on Amazon Bedrock or your own GPU. The entity lever sets what counts as PII: the Extended configuration lifts extended-entity F1 from about 12 percent to about 73 percent across all tested models, without retraining. Because the detection logic is text rather than weights, the same detector adapts to a new domain or backend without a new model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Next steps, if you want to apply this to your own data, follow the walkthrough in the &lt;em&gt;Running the detector end to end&lt;/em&gt; section:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Try the detector:&lt;/strong&gt; Install the package and run the bundled example against a sample of your own corpus to see what it flags.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pick a backend:&lt;/strong&gt; For the highest accuracy with no self-hosting, use a managed model on Amazon Bedrock such as Mistral Large 3 or OSS-GPT 20B. For control over data residency, supply your own adapter for an open model such as OSS-GPT 20B served on your own GPU.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Extend the schema:&lt;/strong&gt; Add your domain-specific entity definitions to the prompt using the Extended configuration, documented in the package repository, as a template, then re-run on your sample.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Close the loop:&lt;/strong&gt; Feed the detected spans, which carry exact character offsets, into a redaction step so cleaned text flows into your training pipeline.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;From there, the natural extensions follow the same pattern. Supporting new entity types or additional languages is an instruction change, not a new model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="resources"&gt;Resources&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The full detection system prompt, the per-dataset label mappings, the complete detector benchmark table, and the Extended-configuration category definitions are documented in the package repository.&lt;/p&gt;
