		<item>
		<title>Deploying Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod with vLLM</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/deploying-qwen3-8-2-4t-a95b-on-amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-with-vllm/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Dmitry Soldatkin]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 09 Sep 2026 22:26:29 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker HyperPod]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">6973e0078540c7d789cc946cb3c7e1fd791a8c2f</guid>

					<description>Learn how to deploy Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B, a 2.4-trillion-parameter open-weight model, on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod with vLLM. This walkthrough covers cluster provisioning, NVFP4 quantization, and an OpenAI-compatible endpoint with built-in reasoning, tool calling, and native MTP speculative decoding.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;On August 12, 2026, Alibaba’s Qwen team released &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B&lt;/a&gt;. This is the first time a Qwen-Max-class model has been made available as open weights. With 2.4 trillion total parameters (95 billion activated per token), a hybrid linear-plus-full-attention architecture, and native context up to 262K tokens (extensible to 1M), Qwen3.8 targets the most demanding agentic and reasoning workloads. These include multi-step coding, long-horizon planning, and autonomous tool use.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Open weights models give you full control. Data stays within your infrastructure, inference behavior can be customized, and there are no per-token API fees at scale. The trade-off is operational: hosting a 2.4T-parameter model requires purpose-built GPU infrastructure and an optimized serving stack.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post we show how to deploy Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B on &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker HyperPod&lt;/a&gt; using &lt;a href="https://docs.vllm.ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;vLLM&lt;/a&gt; on a ml.p6-b300 instance (8× NVIDIA B300 Blackwell Ultra GPUs). We cover the full path from cluster provisioning to an OpenAI-compatible endpoint, including vLLM configuration for NVFP4 quantization, built-in reasoning, tool calling, and native Multi-Token Prediction (MTP) speculative decoding.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This is the second post in our series on deploying open trillion-parameter models on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod. For the first post covering Kimi K3, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/deploying-kimi-k3-on-amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-and-amazon-eks/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Deploying Kimi K3 on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod and Amazon EKS&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="qwen3.8-2.4t-a95b-at-a-glance"&gt;Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B at a glance&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B (the open-weight release of Qwen3.8-Max) is the largest and most capable model in the Qwen family. The following is a summary of the key architectural details relevant to deployment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="architecture"&gt;Architecture&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Attribute&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Value&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Total parameters&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2.4 T&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Activated parameters per token&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;95 B&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Architecture&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Fine-grained Mixture of Experts (MoE)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Expert count&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;512 routed + 1 shared (10 routed experts activated per token)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Layers&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;92&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Layer layout&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3 × (Gated DeltaNet → MoE) → 1 × (Gated Attention → MoE), repeated&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Context window&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;262,144 tokens native. Extensible to 1,010,000&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Max output length&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;128K tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Multi-Token Prediction&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Native MTP draft heads (enables speculative decoding without a separate model)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The hybrid attention design is key to efficient long-context inference. &lt;strong&gt;Gated DeltaNet&lt;/strong&gt; layers (69 of 92) use linear attention with a bounded recurrent state, replacing the growing KV-cache with a fixed-size memory. &lt;strong&gt;Gated Attention&lt;/strong&gt; layers (23 of 92) use full quadratic attention for high-fidelity token interactions. This 3:1 ratio keeps both compute and memory bounded as context scales toward 1M tokens. This is a critical property for agentic workloads that accumulate tool outputs, code, and reasoning traces across many turns.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The fine-grained MoE distributes capacity across 512 small experts rather than a few large ones, improving routing efficiency and specialization. Only approximately 95B parameters are active per forward pass, so serving costs track activated parameters, not the full 2.4T.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="capabilities-and-reasoning-control"&gt;Capabilities and reasoning control&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Qwen3.8 is designed for agentic execution: multi-step coding, autonomous tool use, long-horizon planning, and complex research workflows. It includes built-in reasoning controls through the &lt;code&gt;reasoning_effort&lt;/code&gt; parameter (&lt;code&gt;low&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;medium&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;high&lt;/code&gt;), so developers can trade compute for reasoning depth per request. Dial up for hard multi-step problems and dial down for high-throughput tasks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="model-weights-and-quantization"&gt;Model weights and quantization&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The open weights are published on &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Hugging Face&lt;/a&gt; in the standard Transformers format. Community quantizations include MXFP4 and NVFP4 (W4A4), which compress the model to approximately 1.2 TB, fitting on a single 8-GPU node with B300 Blackwell Ultra GPUs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="benchmark-highlights"&gt;Benchmark highlights&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;According to the vendor’s benchmarking results, Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95 shows particular strength in research workflows (PaperBench 93.0), instruction following (IFBench 82.8), and terminal-based coding (86.6). It performs comparably with leading frontier models across most categories, with remaining headroom on harder repository-level tasks (SWE-bench Pro) and general tool use (Toolathlon). For organizations evaluating self-hosted alternatives to proprietary APIs, these results position Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95 as a credible frontier-class option, particularly for coding agents and research pipelines.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-for-large-moe-inference"&gt;Why Amazon SageMaker HyperPod for large MoE inference&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deploying a 2.4T-parameter model is not only a GPU problem. It requires orchestration that handles model download, container scheduling, health monitoring, autoscaling, and node failures without manual intervention. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker HyperPod&lt;/a&gt; is purpose-built for this class of workload.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/09/ML-21725-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/09/ML-21725-1.png" alt="Architecture diagram of an Amazon SageMaker HyperPod cluster serving Qwen3.8 through vLLM on a p6-b300 node" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: High-level architecture of Amazon SageMaker HyperPod&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;EKS-orchestrated clusters.&lt;/strong&gt; HyperPod clusters use Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS) as the control plane. You get the full Kubernetes landscape (&lt;code&gt;kubectl&lt;/code&gt;, Helm charts, custom resource definitions), while AWS manages the underlying infrastructure lifecycle: networking, storage, GPU driver installation, and the NVIDIA device plugin.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Inference Operator.&lt;/strong&gt; The HyperPod Inference Operator (installed automatically or as an EKS Add-on) provides a single custom resource definition (CRD), &lt;code&gt;InferenceEndpointConfig&lt;/code&gt;, that declaratively specifies your model, container image, GPU resource requests, and vLLM launch arguments. The operator handles:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Model weight download (from Hugging Face Hub, Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3), or Amazon FSx).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Container scheduling and GPU allocation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Health checks and readiness gates.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Rolling updates and endpoint lifecycle management.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Autoscaling through KEDA with Amazon CloudWatch or Prometheus metrics.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Reserved capacity with Flexible Training Plans.&lt;/strong&gt; The &lt;code&gt;ml.p6-b300.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; instance type requires reserved capacity. Flexible Training Plans provide committed GPU reservations that can be allocated directly to your HyperPod cluster. There’s no contention with on-demand pools and no cold-start capacity risk.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Resilience.&lt;/strong&gt; HyperPod continuously monitors node health and automatically replaces degraded nodes. For sustained inference workloads running 24/7, this alleviates the operational overhead of manually detecting and recovering from hardware failures.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Additional inference features&lt;/strong&gt; (Inference Operator v3.x):&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-model-deployment-dpd.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Disaggregated Prefill and Decode&lt;/a&gt; (DPD) – separates prefill and decode onto distinct GPU pools for predictable per-token latency under concurrent load.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-model-deployment-data-capture.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Inference data capture&lt;/a&gt; – log inputs/outputs at the endpoint, load balancer, or pod level.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Local NVMe model deployment – load weights from node-local storage to reduce cold-start latency.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-model-deployment-custom-certs.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Route 53 DNS management&lt;/a&gt; – automatic custom domain records for your endpoints.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In short: You write a YAML manifest describing &lt;em&gt;what&lt;/em&gt; to deploy. HyperPod handles &lt;em&gt;how&lt;/em&gt; to run it reliably at scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="infrastructure-sizing-matching-hardware-to-the-model"&gt;Infrastructure sizing: Matching hardware to the model&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-p6-b300-instance"&gt;The p6-b300 instance&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;ml.p6-b300.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; provides the compute density required for single-node serving of Qwen3.8:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Resource&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Specification&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;GPUs&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;8× NVIDIA B300 (Blackwell Ultra)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;GPU memory&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;288 GB HBM3e per GPU (&lt;strong&gt;2.1 TB total&lt;/strong&gt;)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;GPU memory bandwidth&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;8 TB/s per GPU&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;GPU interconnect&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;NVLink + NVSwitch, 14.4 TB/s bisection bandwidth&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;FP4 compute&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~15 PFLOPS per GPU (120 PFLOPS total)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;vCPUs&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;192 (Intel Xeon Emerald Rapids)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;System memory&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;4,096 GiB&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Networking&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;6,400 Gbps EFA&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Local storage&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3.8 TB NVMe SSD&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="why-nvfp4-quantization"&gt;Why NVFP4 quantization&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At BF16 precision, Qwen3.8’s 2.4T parameters require approximately 4.8 TB of memory for weights alone, exceeding a single 8-GPU node. NVFP4 (W4A4) quantization compresses weights to approximately 4 bits per parameter, bringing the total weight footprint to approximately 1.2 TB. This fits comfortably within the 2.1 TB of aggregate GPU memory on a p6-b300 instance, leaving headroom for KV-cache and activations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="memory-budget"&gt;Memory budget&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A rough breakdown for a single p6-b300 node:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Component&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Estimated Size&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Notes&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Model weights (NVFP4)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~1.2 TB&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2.4T params × 4 bits&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;KV-cache (full attention layers)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Variable&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;23 layers × KV heads × context length&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Recurrent state (DeltaNet layers)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Fixed ~50–100 GB&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;69 layers × bounded state (does not grow with context)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Activations + overhead&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~100–200 GB&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Tensor-parallel buffers, framework overhead&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Available headroom&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;~500–700 GB&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;For batching and longer contexts&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The hybrid attention architecture is a key advantage here: the 69 DeltaNet layers maintain a fixed-size recurrent state regardless of context length, unlike traditional models where KV-cache grows linearly with every layer. Only the 23 full-attention layers contribute to context-dependent memory growth.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="throughput-expectations"&gt;Throughput expectations&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Reference numbers from NVIDIA’s Day-0 benchmarks on GB300 NVL72 (FP8, 72 GPUs): &amp;gt;4K tokens/sec/GPU, &amp;gt;350 tokens/sec/user. A single 8-GPU p6-b300 node with NVFP4 will deliver proportionally lower aggregate throughput but remains well-suited for production inference workloads with moderate concurrency.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="capacity-procurement"&gt;Capacity procurement&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;ml.p6-b300.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; instance type isn’t available on-demand. You must procure capacity through a &lt;strong&gt;Flexible Training Plan&lt;/strong&gt;, a committed reservation of GPU availability for your HyperPod cluster. Set the target Availability Zone to match your plan’s allocation when configuring the instance group.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="vllm-configuration-deep-dive"&gt;vLLM configuration deep dive&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This section details the vLLM serving parameters for Qwen3.8 on a single p6-b300 node. The configuration is informed by the &lt;a href="https://recipes.vllm.ai/Qwen/Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B?hardware=b300&amp;amp;variant=nvfp4&amp;amp;features=tool_calling,reasoning,spec_decoding" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;vLLM recipe for Qwen3.8 on B300 (NVFP4)&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="base-serving-command"&gt;Base serving command&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The full &lt;code&gt;vllm serve&lt;/code&gt; invocation:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;vllm serve Inferact/Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B-NVFP4 \
    --tensor-parallel-size 8 \
    --quantization nvfp4 \
    --load-format fastsafetensors \
    --trust-remote-code \
    --enable-prefix-caching \
    --moe-backend auto \
    --reasoning-parser qwen3 \
    --enable-auto-tool-choice \
    --tool-call-parser qwen3 \
    --speculative-config '{"method":"mtp","num_speculative_tokens":1}' \
    --served-model-name Qwen3.8&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Key flags explained:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;--tensor-parallel-size 8&lt;/code&gt; – shards the model across all 8 B300 GPUs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;--quantization nvfp4&lt;/code&gt; – activates NVIDIA FP4 (W4A4) quantization so the 2.4T model fits in 2.1 TB of GPU memory.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;--load-format fastsafetensors&lt;/code&gt; – uses accelerated weight deserialization for faster cold-start.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;--trust-remote-code&lt;/code&gt; – required for Qwen3.8’s custom modeling code on Hugging Face.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;--enable-prefix-caching&lt;/code&gt; – reuses computed KV-cache across requests that share prompt prefixes. Critical for multi-turn agentic conversations where the system prompt and conversation history repeat.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;--moe-backend auto&lt;/code&gt; – lets vLLM select the optimal MoE dispatch kernel for the hardware.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="reasoning-thinking-mode"&gt;Reasoning (thinking mode)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;--reasoning-parser qwen3&lt;/code&gt; flag extracts reasoning content from the model’s &lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;think&amp;gt;...&amp;lt;/think&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt; output blocks. Key behaviors:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Qwen3.8 reasoning is &lt;strong&gt;enabled by default&lt;/strong&gt; – no extra flag needed on the model side.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The API response separates &lt;code&gt;reasoning_content&lt;/code&gt; (the thinking trace) from &lt;code&gt;content&lt;/code&gt; (the final answer).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;To disable thinking per-request, pass &lt;code&gt;extra_body={"chat_template_kwargs": {"enable_thinking": False}}&lt;/code&gt; in the client call.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Structured output (&lt;code&gt;guided_json&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;guided_regex&lt;/code&gt;) works alongside reasoning – the structured output engine constrains only the &lt;code&gt;content&lt;/code&gt; field.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="tool-calling-function-calling"&gt;Tool calling (function calling)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;--enable-auto-tool-choice&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;--tool-call-parser qwen3&lt;/code&gt; flags enable OpenAI-compatible function calling:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Supports &lt;code&gt;tool_choice&lt;/code&gt; values: &lt;code&gt;auto&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;required&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;none&lt;/code&gt;, and named functions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Tool calls are parsed from the &lt;code&gt;content&lt;/code&gt; field only — the &lt;code&gt;reasoning_content&lt;/code&gt; is not parsed for function calls. This means the model can reason about &lt;em&gt;which&lt;/em&gt; tool to call, then emit the structured call separately.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;When &lt;code&gt;tool_choice="auto"&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;strict: true&lt;/code&gt; is set on a tool definition, vLLM enforces schema-constrained decoding for tool arguments, facilitating valid JSON output.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="speculative-decoding-native-mtp"&gt;Speculative decoding (native MTP)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;--speculative-config '{"method":"mtp","num_speculative_tokens":1}'&lt;/code&gt; flag enables Multi-Token Prediction using Qwen3.8’s built-in draft heads:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Qwen3.8 was trained with MTP – lightweight draft heads are bundled in the model weights. No separate draft model download or configuration is required.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The draft head predicts the next N tokens in parallel, then verifies them in a single forward pass. Accepted tokens skip individual decode steps, increasing throughput.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;num_speculative_tokens: 1&lt;/code&gt; is the safe starting point. Increase to 2–3 for throughput-sensitive workloads once you’ve validated that the acceptance rate remains high (monitor through vLLM’s &lt;code&gt;/metrics&lt;/code&gt; endpoint).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;MTP adds minimal latency overhead on the draft step because the heads reuse the model’s existing hidden states.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="deployment-walkthrough-on-sagemaker-hyperpod"&gt;Deployment walkthrough on SageMaker HyperPod&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The complete deployment manifests and scripts used in this post are available in our &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/tree/main/SageMakerHyperpod/Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before deploying the model, you need a running HyperPod cluster with p6-b300 capacity:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Create a HyperPod cluster with EKS orchestration.&lt;/strong&gt; In the Amazon SageMaker AI console, navigate to &lt;strong&gt;HyperPod Clusters&lt;/strong&gt;, then choose &lt;strong&gt;Create&lt;/strong&gt;. Choose &lt;strong&gt;Orchestrated by Amazon EKS&lt;/strong&gt; and either &lt;strong&gt;Quick Setup&lt;/strong&gt; (default networking and IAM) or &lt;strong&gt;Custom Setup&lt;/strong&gt; with an existing virtual private cloud (VPC) and subnets. Make sure &lt;strong&gt;Use default Helm charts and add-ons&lt;/strong&gt; is selected so the Inference Operator is installed automatically.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Provision a Flexible Training Plan.&lt;/strong&gt; Under the instance group configuration, select &lt;strong&gt;Training plan&lt;/strong&gt; as the capacity source. Create or attach a plan covering &lt;code&gt;ml.p6-b300.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; with the instance count and duration you need. Set the Target Availability Zone to match the plan’s allocation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Add a p6-b300 worker group.&lt;/strong&gt; Add an instance group with &lt;code&gt;ml.p6-b300.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; and at least 1 instance. Wait for the cluster to reach &lt;strong&gt;Active&lt;/strong&gt; state with healthy GPU nodes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Verify access.&lt;/strong&gt; Confirm you can reach the cluster: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;kubectl get nodes
# Expect node(s) with nvidia.com/gpu: 8 capacity&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="inferenceendpointconfig-manifest"&gt;InferenceEndpointConfig manifest&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Apply the following &lt;code&gt;InferenceEndpointConfig&lt;/code&gt; to deploy Qwen3.8 with the vLLM configuration:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;apiVersion: inference.sagemaker.aws.amazon.com/v1
kind: InferenceEndpointConfig
metadata:
  name: qwen38
spec:
  modelName: qwen38
  instanceType: ml.p6-b300.48xlarge
  invocationEndpoint: v1/chat/completions
  replicas: 1
  modelSourceConfig:
    huggingFaceModel:
      modelId: Inferact/Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B-NVFP4
    modelSourceType: huggingface
  worker:
    image: vllm/vllm-openai:qwen38
    modelInvocationPort:
      containerPort: 8000
      name: http
    modelVolumeMount:
      mountPath: /opt/ml/model
      name: model-weights
    resources:
      limits:
        nvidia.com/gpu: 8
      requests:
        nvidia.com/gpu: 8
    args:
      - "--model"
      - "/opt/ml/model"
      - "--serving-model-name"
      - "Qwen3.8"
      - "--linear-backend"
      - "flashinfer_cutedsl"
      - "--trust-remote-code"
      - "--enable-prefix-caching"
      - "--enable-auto-tool-choice"
      - "--tool-call-parser"
      - "qwen3_coder"
      - "--reasoning-parser"
      - "qwen3"
      - "--served-model-name"
      - "Qwen3.8"
      - "--tensor-parallel-size"
      - "8"
    environmentVariables:
      - name: "VLLM_ENGINE_READY_TIMEOUT_S"
        value: "1800"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This manifest is also available in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/tree/main/SageMakerHyperpod/Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="applying-and-monitoring"&gt;Applying and monitoring&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Apply the manifest:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;kubectl apply -f qwen.yaml&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Monitor the deployment progress:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;# Watch the InferenceEndpointConfig status
kubectl get inferenceendpointconfig qwen38 -w

# Check pod status (model download and container startup)
kubectl get pods -l model-name=qwen38

# View vLLM startup logs
kubectl logs -f &amp;lt;pod-name&amp;gt; --tail=100&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The deployment proceeds through these stages: &lt;strong&gt;model download&lt;/strong&gt; (approximately 1.2 TB from Hugging Face, time depends on network bandwidth) then &lt;strong&gt;weight loading&lt;/strong&gt; (fastsafetensors deserialization to GPU memory) then &lt;strong&gt;health checks pass&lt;/strong&gt; and &lt;strong&gt;endpoint ready&lt;/strong&gt;. On a fresh deployment with no cached weights, expect 15–30 minutes for the full sequence. Subsequent restarts with local NVMe caching are significantly faster.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the endpoint shows &lt;code&gt;Ready&lt;/code&gt;, you can send requests to the service:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;# Get the service endpoint
kubectl get svc -l model-name=qwen38

# Quick health check
curl http://&amp;lt;service-endpoint&amp;gt;:8000/health&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="inference-in-action-calling-the-endpoint"&gt;Inference in action: Calling the endpoint&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the endpoint is ready, it exposes an OpenAI-compatible API. You can use the standard OpenAI Python SDK, &lt;code&gt;curl&lt;/code&gt;, or another HTTP client (note that in this deployment example the endpoint isn’t exposed to the public internet).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="basic-chat-completion-with-reasoning"&gt;Basic chat completion (with reasoning)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from openai import OpenAI

client = OpenAI(
    base_url="http://&amp;lt;service-endpoint&amp;gt;:8000/v1",
    api_key="unused",  # vLLM does not require auth by default
)

response = client.chat.completions.create(
    model="Qwen3.8",
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": "Explain the trade-offs of MoE vs dense models for inference."}],
    temperature=0.6,
    top_p=0.95,
)

# Reasoning trace (the model's thinking)
print("Thinking:", response.choices[0].message.reasoning_content)

# Final answer
print("Answer:", response.choices[0].message.content)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To control reasoning depth per request, pass &lt;code&gt;reasoning_effort&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;response = client.chat.completions.create(
    model="Qwen3.8",
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": "What is 2+2?"}],
    extra_body={"chat_template_kwargs": {"enable_thinking": False}},  # disable thinking entirely
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For streaming responses, check for the &lt;code&gt;reasoning_content&lt;/code&gt; attribute on each chunk’s delta:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;stream = client.chat.completions.create(
    model="Qwen3.8",
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": "Write a Python quicksort."}],
    stream=True,
)

for chunk in stream:
    delta = chunk.choices[0].delta
    if hasattr(delta, "reasoning_content") and delta.reasoning_content:
        print(delta.reasoning_content, end="", flush=True)
    elif delta.content:
        print(delta.content, end="", flush=True)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="tool-calling-example"&gt;Tool calling example&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;tools = [{
    "type": "function",
    "function": {
        "name": "get_stock_price",
        "description": "Get the current stock price for a ticker symbol",
        "parameters": {
            "type": "object",
            "properties": {
                "ticker": {"type": "string", "description": "Stock ticker, e.g. 'AMZN'"}
            },
            "required": ["ticker"],
            "additionalProperties": False,
        },
        "strict": True,
    }
}]

response = client.chat.completions.create(
    model="Qwen3.8",
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": "What's Amazon's stock price right now?"}],
    tools=tools,
    tool_choice="auto",
)

# The model reasons internally, then emits a structured tool call
print("Thinking:", response.choices[0].message.reasoning_content)

tool_call = response.choices[0].message.tool_calls[0].function
print(f"Function: {tool_call.name}, Args: {tool_call.arguments}")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With &lt;code&gt;strict: True&lt;/code&gt; set on the tool definition, vLLM makes sure the arguments conform to the JSON schema. No post-validation is needed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="recommended-sampling-parameters"&gt;Recommended sampling parameters&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For most use cases with Qwen3.8:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;temperature=0.6&lt;/code&gt; – balances creativity and coherence. Use &lt;code&gt;0.0&lt;/code&gt; for deterministic outputs (for example, structured extraction).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;top_p=0.95&lt;/code&gt; – standard nucleus sampling.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;top_k=20&lt;/code&gt; – limits vocabulary at each step and reduces degenerate outputs on long generations.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;max_tokens&lt;/code&gt; – set generously when thinking is enabled, since the &lt;code&gt;reasoning_content&lt;/code&gt; counts toward the token budget. For complex reasoning tasks, 32,768–65,536 is a reasonable ceiling.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A &lt;code&gt;curl&lt;/code&gt; example for quick validation:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;curl http://&amp;lt;service-endpoint&amp;gt;:8000/v1/chat/completions \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{
    "model": "Qwen3.8",
    "messages": [{"role": "user", "content": "Hello, Qwen3.8!"}],
    "temperature": 0.6,
    "max_tokens": 256
  }'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="performance-tuning-tips"&gt;Performance tuning tips&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the endpoint running, the following tuning levers let you optimize for your specific workload pattern.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="benchmark-results"&gt;Benchmark results&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We benchmarked Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B on a single p6-b300 instance (8× B300 GPUs) using 512 requests with 1,024 input tokens and 1,024 output tokens at a concurrency of 32. We tested four configurations to isolate the impact of Expert Parallelism (EP) and Multi-Token Prediction (MTP) speculative decoding:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;TP&lt;/strong&gt; – Tensor Parallelism only (TP=8), our baseline.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;TP+MTP&lt;/strong&gt; – TP=8 with native MTP speculative decoding (&lt;code&gt;num_speculative_tokens: 1&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;TP+EP&lt;/strong&gt; – TP=8 with Expert Parallelism enabled.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;TP+EP+MTP&lt;/strong&gt; – TP=8 with both EP and MTP enabled.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/09/ML-21725-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/09/ML-21725-2.png" alt="Performance comparison across configurations" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Benchmark comparison of TP, MTP, and EP configurations on a p6-b300 node&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Key findings (percentage improvement compared to the TP baseline):&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Configuration&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;TTFT Reduction&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Request Latency Reduction&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Output Throughput Increase&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;TP+MTP&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;−58.7%&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;−7.0%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;+6.2%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;TP+EP&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;−3.5%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;−0.4%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;+1.0%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;TP+EP+MTP&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;−59.7%&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;−12.2%&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;+12.6%&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Analysis:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;MTP is the dominant optimization for TTFT.&lt;/strong&gt; Enabling speculative decoding (even with only 1 draft token) cuts time-to-first-token (TTFT) by nearly 59 percent, from 1,244 ms to 513 ms. This is because the MTP draft head predicts the first output tokens in parallel with the final prefill steps, overlapping compute.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;EP alone provides modest gains&lt;/strong&gt; (approximately 3.5 percent TTFT, approximately 1 percent throughput). The benefit is more pronounced at higher concurrency levels where expert routing contention becomes a bottleneck.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The combination of EP+MTP delivers the best overall result&lt;/strong&gt;: 59.7 percent TTFT reduction, 12.2 percent latency reduction, and 12.6 percent higher output throughput. The two optimizations are complementary: EP reduces expert dispatch overhead while MTP reduces decode latency.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Inter-token latency&lt;/strong&gt; (not shown in chart) also improves: 17.97 ms (TP) to 17.33 ms (TP+MTP) to 16.36 ms (TP+EP+MTP), a 9 percent reduction with the full configuration.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Recommendation:&lt;/strong&gt; For production deployments, enable both EP and MTP (&lt;code&gt;--enable-expert-parallel&lt;/code&gt; + &lt;code&gt;--speculative-config '{"method":"mtp","num_speculative_tokens":1}'&lt;/code&gt;). The combined configuration delivers the best latency and throughput profile with minimal additional complexity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prefix-caching"&gt;Prefix caching&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Enabled with &lt;code&gt;--enable-prefix-caching&lt;/code&gt; (already in our config). vLLM’s Automatic Prefix Caching (APC) reuses KV-cache blocks across requests that share the same prompt prefix. This is common in multi-turn agentic conversations where the system prompt and conversation history repeat. For workloads with high prefix overlap, this can reduce time-to-first-token (TTFT) by 50–80 percent on repeated turns. No downside for workloads without prefix sharing. Unused cached blocks are evicted automatically.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="speculative-decoding-mtp-tuning"&gt;Speculative decoding (MTP) tuning&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Our config starts with &lt;code&gt;num_speculative_tokens: 1&lt;/code&gt; (one extra token drafted per step). Tuning guidance:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Increase to 2–3&lt;/strong&gt; for throughput-sensitive, low-concurrency workloads. Each additional speculative token increases the number of tokens that can be accepted per step, but also increases draft overhead and verification cost.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Monitor acceptance rate&lt;/strong&gt; through the vLLM &lt;code&gt;/metrics&lt;/code&gt; endpoint (&lt;code&gt;spec_decode_acceptance_rate&lt;/code&gt;). If acceptance stays above 70–80 percent, increasing &lt;code&gt;num_speculative_tokens&lt;/code&gt; is profitable. Below 50 percent, reduce it or disable speculation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;High-concurrency caveat&lt;/strong&gt;: at high queries per second (QPS), speculative decoding consumes additional GPU compute for drafting and verification. Under saturation, the overhead can reduce aggregate throughput. Consider disabling MTP under heavy batch load and enabling it only for latency-sensitive single-stream requests.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;MTP provides the best gains for &lt;strong&gt;memory-bound decode&lt;/strong&gt; workloads (long outputs, low batch size) — exactly the pattern for agentic reasoning tasks with extended &lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;think&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt; traces.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="memory-and-context-length"&gt;Memory and context length&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;--gpu-memory-utilization&lt;/code&gt; (default: 0.9) controls how much GPU memory vLLM pre-allocates for KV-cache. For Qwen3.8 with NVFP4, the model weights occupy approximately 57 percent of GPU memory, leaving approximately 43 percent (roughly 900 GB) for cache and overhead. Tuning:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Keep at 0.9 for maximum throughput (more KV-cache slots = more concurrent requests).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Reduce to 0.85 if you observe out-of-memory (OOM) errors during long-context requests. This sacrifices batch capacity but helps prevent preemption cascades.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;--max-model-len&lt;/code&gt; caps the maximum sequence length vLLM will accept. Setting this lower than the model’s full 262K context gives vLLM more KV-cache slots for shorter requests, improving concurrency. Set it to match your actual workload’s maximum context requirement. For example, &lt;code&gt;32,768&lt;/code&gt; for typical coding agents or &lt;code&gt;131,072&lt;/code&gt; for long-document analysis.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="batching-and-scheduling"&gt;Batching and scheduling&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;--max-num-seqs&lt;/code&gt; (default: 256) limits concurrent sequences in a batch. For MoE models with large per-token compute, reducing this to 64–128 decreases scheduling overhead and makes sure each request gets sufficient GPU attention, improving per-request latency at the cost of aggregate throughput.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;--max-num-batched-tokens&lt;/code&gt; controls the total token budget per scheduling step (prefill + decode combined). vLLM V1 uses chunked prefill by default. Large prefills are split into chunks and interleaved with decode steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Lower values (for example, 8192) provide better inter-token latency (ITL), because decodes aren’t stalled by large prefills.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Higher values (for example, 32768+) lead to better TTFT, since more prefill tokens are processed per batch.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For agentic workloads with mixed short and long requests, start at 16384 and adjust based on observed P99 ITL.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="moe-backend-selection"&gt;MoE backend selection&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;--moe-backend auto&lt;/code&gt; lets vLLM choose the optimal kernel. On Blackwell GPUs, this typically selects fused MoE kernels that treat sparse expert dispatch as grouped-GEMM operations, minimizing per-expert scheduling overhead. If profiling (using &lt;code&gt;nsys&lt;/code&gt; or vLLM’s built-in profiler) reveals MoE dispatch as a bottleneck, you can experiment with explicit backends, but &lt;code&gt;auto&lt;/code&gt; is the recommended starting point.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="expert-parallelism-advanced"&gt;Expert parallelism (advanced)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For deployments needing higher concurrency than a single TP=8 configuration allows, vLLM supports Expert Parallelism (EP) where MoE experts are distributed across GPUs independently of attention heads. On a single p6-b300 node, you could combine TP=4 + EP=2. This halves the number of experts per GPU, freeing memory for larger KV-cache at the cost of increased all-to-all communication during expert dispatch. Profile before adopting: on NVLink-connected GPUs the communication cost is low, but gains depend on your concurrency target.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="monitoring"&gt;Monitoring&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;vLLM exposes Prometheus metrics at &lt;code&gt;/metrics&lt;/code&gt;. Key gauges to watch:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;vllm:num_requests_running&lt;/code&gt; – current batch size. Spikes indicate queuing.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;vllm:gpu_cache_usage_perc&lt;/code&gt; – KV-cache utilization. Sustained &amp;gt;95 percent signals preemption risk.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;vllm:num_preemptions_total&lt;/code&gt; – if this climbs, increase &lt;code&gt;gpu_memory_utilization&lt;/code&gt; or reduce &lt;code&gt;max_num_seqs&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;vllm:spec_decode_acceptance_rate&lt;/code&gt; – MTP acceptance rate. Tune &lt;code&gt;num_speculative_tokens&lt;/code&gt; accordingly.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;vllm:e2e_request_latency_seconds&lt;/code&gt; – end-to-end latency distribution for service level agreement (SLA) tracking.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For HyperPod deployments, these metrics can be scraped by Amazon Managed Prometheus and visualized in Grafana, or fed into KEDA for autoscaling decisions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, remove the resources in reverse order:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;# Delete the inference endpoint
kubectl delete inferenceendpointconfig qwen38

# (Optional) Scale down or delete the HyperPod cluster
# via the SageMaker AI console or CLI&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you no longer need GPU capacity, release or let your Flexible Training Plan expire. Cluster deletion stops compute charges. The training plan commitment remains until its term ends regardless of usage.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion-and-next-steps"&gt;Conclusion and next steps&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post we deployed Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B, a 2.4-trillion-parameter open-weight model, on a single p6-b300 instance using Amazon SageMaker HyperPod and vLLM. The deployment exposes a production-ready OpenAI-compatible endpoint with built-in reasoning, tool calling, and native MTP speculative decoding, all managed through a single Kubernetes manifest.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;From here you can:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Benchmark with your workload&lt;/strong&gt; – run &lt;code&gt;vllm bench serve&lt;/code&gt; or your own evaluation harness against the endpoint to establish baseline throughput and latency for your specific prompt distribution.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Integrate into agentic pipelines&lt;/strong&gt; – connect the endpoint to your orchestration framework (LangChain, CrewAI, or custom) using the standard OpenAI SDK. The tool-calling and reasoning outputs are compatible without additional configuration.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Fine-tune for your domain&lt;/strong&gt; – use NVIDIA NeMo or Amazon SageMaker training jobs to produce domain-adapted checkpoints, then redeploy with the same manifest by swapping the model ID.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Add guardrails&lt;/strong&gt; – layer I/O filtering (for example, NVIDIA NeMo Guardrails or a custom proxy) in front of the vLLM endpoint for content safety and policy compliance.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt;

  &lt;p style="overflow: hidden"&gt;Dmitry is a Worldwide Leader for Specialist Solutions Architecture, SageMaker Inference at AWS. He leads efforts to help customers design, build, and optimize GenAI and AI/ML solutions across the enterprise. His work spans a wide range of ML use cases, with a primary focus on Generative AI, deep learning, and deploying ML at scale. He has partnered with companies across industries including financial services, insurance, and telecommunications.&lt;/p&gt;

 &lt;div class="blog-author-box" style="padding-top: 2.0em"&gt;

		<item>
		<title>ICYMI: What landed for AI builders in August 2026</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/icymi-what-landed-for-ai-builders-in-august-2026/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Tanvi Girinath]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 09 Sep 2026 20:01:03 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">5a4826a00b8c3e2362294bd88790a97f7dfeae72</guid>

					<description>A recap of August 2026 launches for AI builders across Amazon Bedrock, Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, and Strands: million-token context for OpenAI models, cross-Region inference, agents that run for up to 14 days on dedicated compute, expanded AWS GovCloud availability, and Strands Robots for physical deployment.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;A recap of the biggest Amazon Bedrock, AgentCore, and Strands updates from August 2026.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At AWS, we have long focused on making foundational technologies accessible and providing the infrastructure needed to put them to work. &lt;span&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/span&gt;, used by more than 225,000 active customers, including over 80% of Fortune 100 companies, embodies that commitment by providing access to leading models and a broad set of tools to build and scale AI, with the security, reliability, performance, and cost efficiency customers need in production. As agentic applications expand, Amazon Bedrock extends this foundation with &lt;span&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/span&gt;, which lets you build, connect, and optimize agents using any framework and model. AWS also released the &lt;span&gt;&lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;Strands Agent Harness SDK&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/span&gt; as open source, giving you the flexibility to create agents and deploy them wherever you choose. Together, they help you turn model intelligence into production agents without limiting how those agents are built or where they run.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As models become more capable, organizations can ask a more ambitious question: how much meaningful work can AI take on from start to finish? Across industries, agents are moving into workflows that require them to reason over enterprise information, remain productive over longer time horizons, and act across digital and physical systems. As the scope of that work expands, attention is shifting to the complete system around the model, including what context it can access, what actions it can take, where data is processed, and how its decisions and costs are governed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We believe the next phase of AI will be shaped not only by what models can do, but by how confidently you can delegate responsibility to the systems built around them. That confidence depends on systems that can understand the task in front of them, persist as it unfolds, and operate within clear organizational boundaries. August’s updates move in that direction, giving you stronger foundations for AI systems that can understand more, work longer, and take on demanding responsibility across enterprise, regulated, and physical environments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Reason over more context at scale with greater control&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Bring entire working sets and current web context into your OpenAI applications on Amazon Bedrock. &lt;/strong&gt;GPT-5.6 Sol, Terra, and Luna now support &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/gpt-sol-terra-luna-long-context-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;million-token context windows&lt;/a&gt; with prompt caching, helping reduce cost and latency when context is reused. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/amazon-bedrock-web/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Web Search&lt;/a&gt; lets the models find current information beyond their training data, incorporate relevant results into their answers, and provide citations without requiring you to integrate a separate search provider. For use cases that need the freshest details, such as live pricing or newly released documentation, the models can also &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/amazon-bedrock-web-access-web-search/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;retrieve content directly&lt;/a&gt; from public websites. Together, these capabilities let you analyze an entire codebase or regulatory document, check its contents against the latest public information, and return a cited response from a single API call.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/amazon-bedrock-cross-region-openai-v2/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Route inference globally&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;strong&gt; without stitching together Regions.&lt;/strong&gt; With cross-Region inference, you can access GPT-5.6 Sol, Terra, and Luna from more than 25 AWS Regions. Global profiles give you the broadest capacity at lower per-token prices, while Geo profiles keep inference processing within a defined geography. With these updates, you can increase throughput during demand spikes while choosing the geographic boundary that fits your application’s data-processing requirements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Understand and control inference spend as model usage grows.&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/amazon-bedrock-expands-iam-principal-cost-allocation-bedrock-mantle/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;IAM principal cost allocation&lt;/a&gt; helps you attribute inference spend to a user, team, project, application, or cost center, while &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/aws-cost-anomaly-detection-bedrock-3P/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Cost Anomaly Detection&lt;/a&gt; now monitors third-party foundation model spend on Amazon Bedrock and provides root-cause breakdowns when costs change unexpectedly. OpenAI also announced lower pricing across the GPT-5.6 family on Amazon Bedrock, including &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/07/openai-gpt-terra-luna-pricing-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Luna, Terra,&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/bedrock-openai-gpt-56-sol-reduced-pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Sol&lt;/a&gt;. This gives you greater visibility into which teams are driving spend, helps you catch unusual changes earlier, and supports more informed decisions as AI workloads scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Faster cyber defense from detection to response&amp;nbsp; &lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Give security teams frontier AI for both defensive and authorized offensive workflows.&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/openai-daybreak-red-and-blue-on-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Daybreak Red and Daybreak Blue from OpenAI&lt;/a&gt; are now available to eligible customers on Amazon Bedrock. Daybreak Blue supports defensive work such as vulnerability discovery, detection engineering, and incident response. Daybreak Red supports advanced, authorized tasks such as vulnerability research, exploit reproduction, and mitigation development. You can now investigate and address vulnerabilities faster while applying stronger identity verification, monitoring, access controls, and zero-operator-access infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Get more done with agents that stay on track&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Keep production agents running long enough to complete multi-day work.&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/aws-bedrock-agentcore-runtime-instances-generally-available/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore runtime instances&lt;/a&gt; let you run agents on dedicated Amazon EC2 compute, including GPU-accelerated, memory-optimized, and compute-optimized instances, with sessions lasting up to 14 days. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/bedrock-agentcore-two-new-regions/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore also expanded&lt;/a&gt; to US West (N. California) and Asia Pacific (Hyderabad). You can now hand agents long-running research, coding, and monitoring work, match each workload to the compute it needs, and run it closer to the users and systems it serves.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Let agents act more independently while keeping their behavior and spending within defined boundaries.&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/temporal-policies-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Temporal policies&lt;/a&gt; evaluate each action against what an agent has already done, letting you enforce sequences, prerequisites, approval gates, matching values between calls, and data freshness. Rate limiting controls requests, inference tokens, and concurrent connections by user or group. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/bedrock-agentcore-payments-ga/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore payments&lt;/a&gt; lets agents access and pay for APIs, MCP resources, and paid content with infrastructure-enforced spending limits and end-to-end observability. Together, these updates let you give agents more independence while controlling what can happen, in what order, at what rate, and within what budget.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Give agents timely context without weakening data boundaries.&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/web-search-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Web Search in AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; lets agents include or exclude domains and filter results by publication date, helping them retrieve current information from approved sources. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/agentcore-memory-json-payloads" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore memory&lt;/a&gt; can extract long-term memories from activity logs, behavioral events, system events, and other structured JSON data, not only from conversations. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/agentcorememory-fine-grained-access-control" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Fine-grained access control &lt;/a&gt;can then isolate those memories by user or tenant. Now you can ground agents in trusted sources, build memory from business events, and keep that context isolated without adding custom authorization logic to every application.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Find and reuse approved agents and tools instead of rebuilding what already exists&lt;/strong&gt;. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/manage-agents-tools-and-skills-at-scale-with-aws-agent-registry/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Agent Registry&lt;/a&gt; gives you a searchable, governed catalog for agents, MCP servers, skills, and custom resources. Organization-wide detection can identify agents on AgentCore Runtime and MCP servers on AgentCore Gateway across connected AWS accounts, while approved resources can also surface in Amazon Quick. You can find trusted capabilities by intent or name, reuse them across workflows, and reduce agent sprawl and duplicative development.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Build with more choice in regulated environments&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Bring advanced models, agent capabilities, and cross-modal retrieval to regulated workloads&lt;/strong&gt;. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/07/claude-opus-5-aws-govcloud/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Claude Opus 5 &lt;/a&gt;is now available in AWS GovCloud (US) Regions with zero data retention enabled by default. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/openai-gpt-terra-luna-govcloud/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenAI GPT-5.6 Terra and Luna&lt;/a&gt; are also available in AWS GovCloud (US) Regions, with million-token context windows and prompt caching. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/amazon-nova-mme-govcloud/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Nova Multimodal Embeddings&lt;/a&gt; brings retrieval across text, documents, images, video, and audio to AWS GovCloud (US-West), while &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/agentcore-memory-policy-harness-govcloud/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore memory, policy, and the managed harness&lt;/a&gt; add managed context, controls, and orchestration. This helps you build coding agents, document-analysis systems, multimodal retrieval applications, and governed agent workflows in AWS GovCloud using more of the same capabilities available in commercial Regions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Extend AI agents into the physical world&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Move from robot demonstrations to training and physical deployment through one connected workflow&lt;/strong&gt;. &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/blog/amazon/strands-lerobot-streaming-data-loop/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Robots connects Strands Agents, LeRobot, and Hugging Face Storage Buckets&lt;/a&gt;, letting you record demonstrations, stream datasets in the LeRobot format, train policies, and deploy them to simulated or physical hardware without converting the underlying data. This allows you to iterate on robotics policies using one data format and one agent workflow from simulation through physical deployment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Coordinate multiple robots and devices without rebuilding the agent for each environment. &lt;/strong&gt;&lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/blog/robots-working-together-model-hardware-standard-strands-robots/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Robots supports mesh-based discovery and coordination&lt;/a&gt; across simulated and physical devices. Zenoh connects robots on the same local network, while AWS IoT Core supports geographically distributed fleets. Strands Robots and AWS are also participating in the limited research preview of the Model Hardware Standard. This makes it easier for you to prototype multi-robot workflows in simulation, move them across local and cloud-connected fleets with fewer code changes, and explore standardized controls for physical equipment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Get started&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Explore &lt;a href="https://aws-samples.github.io/sample-amazon-bedrock-central/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;, deploy agents with the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agentcore-get-started-cli.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore CLI&lt;/a&gt;, or build your first agent with the &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Harness SDK&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Interested in learning how Amazon Bedrock can support your team? &lt;/em&gt;&lt;a href="https://pages.awscloud.com/Amazon-Bedrock-Contact-Us.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;em&gt;Connect with us&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;em&gt; to start the conversation.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;About the authors&lt;/h2&gt;

 &lt;div class="blog-author-box" style="padding-top: 2.0em"&gt;

		<item>
		<title>How Heurist Finance built an AI-native investment workbench on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-heurist-finance-built-an-ai-native-investment-workbench-on-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[JW Wang]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 09 Sep 2026 18:11:12 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">8b2b6f1fc936da150ec6ba65d555af391dc31a8e</guid>

					<description>Learn how Heurist built Heurist Finance, a conversational AI investment workbench, on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. This customer story shows how AgentCore payments, Identity, Memory, Code Interpreter, and Observability let a small team buy premium market data per query, isolate analysis in a sandbox, and keep every action auditable.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.heurist.ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Heurist&lt;/a&gt; uses &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; to build AI-powered financial intelligence for retail investors. Its flagship product, Heurist Finance, brings several institutional-style workflows into one chat experience: it gathers market data, reads filings and news, runs deep research, builds and stress-tests portfolios, and monitors positions. Each answer reflects the user’s portfolio and preferences. Heurist’s goal is to make tools such as unified risk-and-return views, whole-portfolio construction, and scenario analysis accessible to anyone with a market question.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post explains how Heurist built the system behind Heurist Finance on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, using &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/payments.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore payments&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, to buy premium data per query. It shows how paid data access, sandboxed analysis, identity, memory, and observability come together in an auditable response.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="business-challenge"&gt;Business challenge&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Heurist gives retail investors access to research built on premium market, macroeconomic, fundamental, and alternative data. Those sources sit behind paywalls and bespoke APIs. No single vendor covers them all, and enterprise contracts are difficult to justify before a product has a large user base. Buying only the data required for each question offered a better economic model, but it introduced another problem. The agent would need to spend funds on a user’s behalf while enforcing custody, spending limits, and audit requirements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This creates a broader production challenge. Every action had to map to a specific user, session, and request, so identity needed to persist across the full workflow. The team also needed cross-session state, isolated code execution, payment orchestration, and end-to-end tracing. Building that infrastructure in-house could take months and divert the team from the research workflows and personalization that differentiate Heurist Finance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Heurist deployed its agents on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale with any framework or model. Heurist orchestrates its agents with &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands&lt;/a&gt; and uses &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/anthropic/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Anthropic Claude&lt;/a&gt;, available on Amazon Bedrock. Figure 1 shows how the surrounding AgentCore services support each request.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/27/ML-21623-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/27/ML-21623-1.png" alt="Architecture diagram of Heurist Finance on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. A Strands orchestrator connects user portfolio data in Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL to Anthropic Claude on Amazon Bedrock and to AgentCore Identity, memory, Code Interpreter, Observability, and payments. Analysis artifacts go to Amazon S3, traces go to Amazon CloudWatch, credentials remain in AWS Secrets Manager, and paid x402 requests settle in USDC on Base." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Heurist Finance solution architecture on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 1 shows the Strands orchestrator calling Anthropic Claude on Amazon Bedrock, loading portfolio data from &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/rds/aurora/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL&lt;/a&gt;, and coordinating AgentCore &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity-overview.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Identity&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/memory.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Memory&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/code-interpreter-tool.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Code Interpreter&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/observability.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Observability&lt;/a&gt;, and payments. Analysis artifacts go to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)&lt;/a&gt;, and traces go to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt;. Credentials remain in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/secrets-manager/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Secrets Manager&lt;/a&gt;, while AgentCore payments connects paid data requests to USDC stablecoin settlement on the &lt;a href="https://www.base.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Base&lt;/a&gt; blockchain network.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="answering-a-question-across-paid-data"&gt;Answering a question across paid data&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A single user turn can combine prices, macroeconomic indicators, filings, fundamentals, and news, then run correlations, scenario analysis, charts, or backtests over the results. AgentCore Code Interpreter, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, performs that work in an isolated sandbox with no arbitrary network egress. The sandbox runs in the AWS Cloud and tears down when the analysis finishes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Some of that data costs money, and Heurist Finance buys it per query through Amazon Bedrock AgentCore payments. A Payment Manager coordinates a &lt;code&gt;CoinbaseCDP&lt;/code&gt; Payment Connector. Each interaction receives a Payment Session with a &lt;code&gt;maxSpendAmount&lt;/code&gt; value that caps spending for that run and a Payment Instrument (an embedded crypto wallet) scoped to Base.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each paid request follows the x402 protocol:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Heurist Finance requests a paid data feed from a merchant.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The merchant returns HTTP 402 with x402 payment terms: amount, recipient, asset (USDC), and network (Base).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AgentCore payments checks the payload against the Payment Session’s &lt;code&gt;maxSpendAmount&lt;/code&gt; value. If the charge would exceed the cap, Heurist Finance tells the user and suggests alternatives.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Within budget, Heurist Finance calls the Process Payment API, which signs the payment through the Payment Instrument. Credentials are retrieved at runtime from AWS Secrets Manager.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Heurist Finance retries the request with proof in the &lt;code&gt;X-PAYMENT&lt;/code&gt; header, and the merchant returns the data.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With x402, Heurist Finance buys only the data a question needs, without a vendor contract or prepayment. Heurist implements the flow with AgentCore payments, a Payment Manager, a Payment Connector, and SDK integration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="research-that-knows-the-investor"&gt;Research that knows the investor&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a user asks a broad question, such as whether a specific stock is overvalued, Heurist Finance grounds the research in the user’s holdings, watchlist, time horizon, and risk preferences. The answer builds on prior context instead of starting from zero and becomes more specific as the profile develops.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore memory, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, stores the user’s preferences, thesis state, and conversation history across sessions. AgentCore Identity, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, scopes that store to one user, so Heurist does not need to build a separate preference store and access-control layer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="isolation-security-and-audit"&gt;Isolation, security, and audit&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A user’s profile and conversation history can reveal beliefs, risk tolerance, time horizon, and positions. Heurist therefore treats identity, access, and audit as part of every request.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore Identity carries the authenticated user through every service call. Each tool call, payment, and memory operation records the user ID, workload identity, request ID, and trace ID, creating one audit trail across services.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each service also enforces its own boundary. AgentCore Identity accepts OAuth and issues scoped credentials. AgentCore payments scopes the Payment Session and Payment Instrument per user, AgentCore memory binds profile and conversation data to one user, and AgentCore Code Interpreter isolates the analysis runtime.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails filters both input and output. Input filters help block prompt-injection attempts aimed at payment and data tools, while output filters help enforce Heurist’s policy against recommending an unhedged single stock. Payment credentials stay in AWS Secrets Manager, where the Payment Credential Provider retrieves them at runtime for the Payment Connector.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="request-flow-one-user-question"&gt;Request flow: One user question&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Consider the question, “How does today’s PCE release impact my portfolio?” The orchestrator loads the user’s portfolio from Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL, with AgentCore Identity scoping the read to that user. The orchestrator then calls a paid consensus-forecast endpoint. After receiving HTTP 402, AgentCore payments checks the Payment Session spend cap and signs the payment through the Payment Instrument. The orchestrator retries the request with proof in the &lt;code&gt;X-PAYMENT&lt;/code&gt; header.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore Code Interpreter computes the portfolio impact and writes a chart to Amazon S3 from within its sandbox. Amazon Bedrock synthesizes the answer using the user’s holdings, time horizon, and risk preferences, and the response streams back with the chart attached. Figure 2 traces this sequence end to end.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/27/ML-21623-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/27/ML-21623-2.png" alt="Sequence diagram for the question ‘How does today’s PCE release impact my portfolio?’ Heurist Finance loads the user profile, purchases consensus data through AgentCore payments and x402, computes portfolio impact in AgentCore Code Interpreter, stores a chart in Amazon S3, and streams the answer." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Request flow for a single user question, from profile lookup to paid data purchase to streamed answer&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The example brings Amazon Aurora, AgentCore Identity, AgentCore payments, AgentCore Code Interpreter, Amazon S3, Amazon Bedrock, and AgentCore Observability, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, into one workflow. Shared user and trace context connects the paid data purchase to the resulting portfolio analysis.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="outcome"&gt;Outcome&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Heurist estimates roughly 80% less agent-system engineering than an in-house large language model (LLM) orchestration stack, because AgentCore manages identity, cross-session memory, sandboxing, and payments infrastructure. It also provides Heurist with predictable per-user marginal costs that support retail pricing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture also provides two operational properties:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AgentCore Observability traces make agent decisions reproducible, allowing compliance questions to be resolved in a single query, increasing compliance readiness and decreasing troubleshooting time.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Payment credentials remain in AWS Secrets Manager and are retrieved at runtime by the Payment Connector, increasing safety for end users.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“AgentCore does the platform work so we can double down our energy on the product work. The managed infrastructure saved us months.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— JW Wang, Founder of Heurist&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Heurist’s experience shows how managed agent infrastructure can let a small team focus on differentiated financial research rather than the system beneath it. AgentCore payments is central to that model: it gives the product governed, auditable access to paid data without requiring Heurist to build payment infrastructure from scratch.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="whats-next"&gt;What’s next&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With that foundation in place, Heurist is extending the product in three directions: event-driven research tied to earnings calendars, portfolio-aware analysis of market events, and recommendations based on what traders with similar horizons are researching.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="get-started"&gt;Get started&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore payments is available in the regions listed&amp;nbsp;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agentcore-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;here&lt;/a&gt;. To learn more, visit the&amp;nbsp;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/payments.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore payments documentation&lt;/a&gt;&amp;nbsp;or the&amp;nbsp;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/amazon-bedrock-agentcore-payments-is-now-generally-available-enabling-agents-to-transact-safely-and-autonomously-at-scale/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS News Blog&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr&gt;
