			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Simplify and support your TorchServe workloads using Ray Serve Deep Learning Containers</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/simplify-and-support-your-torchserve-workloads-using-ray-serve-deep-learning-containers/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Ananth Raghavendra]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 09 Sep 2026 15:51:29 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">40560bc1165354abd0304d5c35c0cd427ba27c99</guid>

					<description>TorchServe is no longer maintained, leaving teams to own the entire GPU inference stack. The AWS Ray Serve Deep Learning Container is a supported, pre-tested container with the framework, GPU drivers, and serving layer already assembled. This post walks through deploying a vision-language model on Amazon EKS using the Ray Serve DLC on a single GPU node.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://pytorch.org/serve/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;TorchServe&lt;/a&gt; is no longer actively maintained. The official project notice states there are no planned updates, bug fixes, new features, or security patches, and that vulnerabilities might not be addressed. For teams that run model inference on TorchServe today, this means security patches stop and compatibility updates with newer versions of PyTorch and CUDA stop. Engineers are left owning the entire dependency chain themselves: choosing compatible versions across the GPU stack, patching vulnerabilities in every layer, and debugging subtle failures when any component drifts out of alignment. This is undifferentiated work that slows model delivery without adding value to the final product.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/machine-learning/containers/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Deep Learning Containers&lt;/a&gt; (DLCs) have long addressed this kind of problem for training workloads. DLCs are pre-built, performance-optimized Docker images that bundle a framework, its dependencies, and the GPU stack into a tested, patched combination you can pull and use immediately. With the launch of the &lt;a href="https://gallery.ecr.aws/deep-learning-containers/ray" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Ray Serve DLC&lt;/a&gt;, that same approach now extends to inference. You get a container purpose-built for serving models behind an HTTP endpoint, maintained and tested by AWS, with the full inference stack already assembled.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post introduces the Ray Serve DLC and walks through deploying a vision-language model on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eks/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service&lt;/a&gt; (Amazon EKS) using this new image. We cover running a model with the Ray Serve DLC, running the serving application with &lt;a href="https://docs.ray.io/en/latest/serve/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Ray Serve&lt;/a&gt;, and deploying it on a single GPU node. The complete code is available in the accompanying repository.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To follow along with this post, you need:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with billing enabled.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Sufficient service quotas for &lt;code&gt;g5.xlarge&lt;/code&gt; instances in your target Region.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI), &lt;code&gt;eksctl&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;kubectl&lt;/code&gt; installed and configured.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="compose-ray-serve-application"&gt;Compose Ray Serve application&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Ray Serve DLC for CPU is built on the Amazon Linux 2023 base image. The GPU variant is built on the official NVIDIA Amazon Linux 2023 image, which includes both the OS layer and the CUDA runtime libraries. On top of this foundation, the DLC adds a deep learning framework (PyTorch), the Ray Serve serving layer with FastAPI and Uvicorn, and common utilities for vision, audio, and multimodal workloads. These utilities include FFmpeg compiled with NVIDIA hardware acceleration for video preprocessing. All components are validated and tested together before each release, so there’s no version drift between the CUDA runtime, the framework, and the serving layer. Security patches are applied at build time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Ray Serve DLC is published as separate images for Amazon EKS and Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2), and for Amazon SageMaker, each with a dedicated entrypoint suited to that environment’s serving contract. Both share the same underlying stack and dependencies. For the current list of available image tags, see the &lt;a href="https://aws.github.io/deep-learning-containers/reference/available_images/#ray-serve" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Ray DLC availale images page&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The DLC ships the common inference stack, so many models, including the Qwen3-VL model, run on it without a custom image. For models that need extra libraries, engineers can layer them on the same tested base.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we use the GPU version of the Ray Serve DLC to serve the &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Qwen3-VL-2B&lt;/a&gt; vision-language model. The application is injected into the container through a ConfigMap, which keeps the deployment flexible so you can change the serving code without rebuilding the image. DLC already provides the GPU stack, PyTorch, Ray Serve, and Transformers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="write-the-serving-application"&gt;Write the serving application&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With Ray Serve, a model endpoint is a Python class decorated with &lt;a href="https://docs.ray.io/en/latest/serve/api/doc/ray.serve.deployment.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;span class="citation" data-cites="serve.deployment"&gt;@serve.deployment&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. You implement &lt;code&gt;__call__&lt;/code&gt; to handle HTTP requests and call &lt;code&gt;.bind()&lt;/code&gt; to register it. There’s no model archiver, no handler class hierarchy, and no properties configuration file. If you’re coming from TorchServe, this replaces the custom handler, the &lt;code&gt;torch-model-archiver&lt;/code&gt; step, and the &lt;code&gt;config.properties&lt;/code&gt; file.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following example loads the &lt;code&gt;Qwen3-VL-2B&lt;/code&gt; vision-language model onto the GPU and exposes it as an HTTP endpoint. When a request arrives with an image URL and a text prompt, the model generates a natural-language response describing or answering questions about the image:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from ray import serve
from transformers import AutoModelForImageTextToText, AutoProcessor
import torch

@serve.deployment(ray_actor_options={"num_gpus": 1})
class QwenVLService:
    def __init__(self):
        model_name = "Qwen/Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct"
        self.processor = AutoProcessor.from_pretrained(model_name)
        self.model = AutoModelForImageTextToText.from_pretrained(
            model_name, torch_dtype=torch.float16
        ).to("cuda")

    async def __call__(self, request):
        try:
            body = await request.json()
            image_url = body.get("image_url")
            prompt = body.get("prompt")
            messages = [{"role": "user", "content": [
                {"type": "image", "image": image_url},
                {"type": "text", "text": prompt},
            ]}]
            inputs = self.processor.apply_chat_template(
                messages, tokenize=True, add_generation_prompt=True,
                return_dict=True, return_tensors="pt"
            ).to("cuda")
            generated_ids = self.model.generate(**inputs, max_new_tokens=200)
            trimmed = [o[len(i):] for i, o in zip(inputs.input_ids, generated_ids)]
            output = self.processor.batch_decode(trimmed, skip_special_tokens=True)[0]
            return {"response": output}
        Except Exception:
            logger.exception("Qwen inference request failed")
            return {"error": "Unable to generate a response. Please try again later."}

app = QwenVLService.bind()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The entire serving logic in &lt;code&gt;qwen_serve.py&lt;/code&gt; is added to a &lt;code&gt;qwen-serve-code&lt;/code&gt; ConfigMap. The &lt;code&gt;@serve.deployment&lt;/code&gt; decorator with &lt;code&gt;ray_actor_options={"num_gpus": 1}&lt;/code&gt; tells Ray to schedule this deployment on a worker with one available GPU. The model loads in &lt;code&gt;float16&lt;/code&gt; to fit within the 24 GB of VRAM available on the A10G GPU used in the next section.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="deploy-on-amazon-eks"&gt;Deploy on Amazon EKS&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deploy the Ray Serve DLC along with the &lt;code&gt;qwen-serve-code&lt;/code&gt; ConfigMap created earlier. The following diagram shows the target architecture: an Amazon EKS cluster with a single GPU node running one pod that serves the model over HTTP on port 8000.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/08/ML-21647-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/08/ML-21647-1.png" alt="Architecture diagram of an Amazon EKS cluster with a single GPU node running one pod that serves the model over HTTP on port 8000" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Single-node inference architecture on Amazon EKS, with one GPU pod serving the model over HTTP on port 8000&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This deployment uses a single &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ec2/instance-types/g5/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;g5.xlarge&lt;/a&gt; instance (one NVIDIA A10G GPU, 24 GB VRAM). It’s a single-node inference setup: one pod, one GPU, one machine. For multi-node distributed serving (model parallelism across machines or horizontal autoscaling with multiple replicas), you would build on top of this foundation using &lt;a href="https://docs.ray.io/en/latest/cluster/kubernetes/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;KubeRay&lt;/a&gt; to orchestrate Ray workers across nodes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The accompanying repository includes scripts that automate the infrastructure setup:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;deploy_cluster.sh&lt;/code&gt; provisions an EKS cluster using &lt;code&gt;eksctl&lt;/code&gt;, with virtual private cloud (VPC) networking, an OIDC provider for AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)-based pod authentication, and core cluster add-ons.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;deploy_node_group.sh&lt;/code&gt; adds a managed GPU node group with a single &lt;code&gt;g5.xlarge&lt;/code&gt; instance labeled &lt;code&gt;role=gpu-worker&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;deploy_ray_cluster.sh&lt;/code&gt; applies a ConfigMap with &lt;code&gt;qwen_serve.py&lt;/code&gt;, deploys a Kubernetes Deployment manifest that schedules the pod on the GPU node, and starts Ray Serve on port 8000.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;After running the three scripts, verify that the pod is running and the GPU is allocated: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;kubectl get pods -n inference
kubectl describe pod -n inference -l app=ray-serve | grep "nvidia.com/gpu"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the pod reporting ready, port-forward to it so we can test it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;kubectl port-forward -n inference deploy/ray-cluster 8000:8000&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On a new terminal, send a request that asks the &lt;code&gt;Qwen3-VL-2B&lt;/code&gt; vision-language model to describe an image:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;curl -X POST http://127.0.0.1:8000/ \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{
    "image_url": "https://s3.amazonaws.com/model-server/inputs/kitten.jpg",
    "prompt": "Describe this image briefly."
  }'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You should receive a JSON response with the model’s description of the image. To confirm that inference is running on the GPU:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;kubectl exec -n inference deploy/ray-cluster -- nvidia-smi&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; The pod reports Ready a minute or two before Ray Serve starts answering, because the model is still loading. If the first request is refused, wait a minute and try the request again.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, tear down the resources in reverse order:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;./delete_ray_cluster.sh   # Remove the Ray Serve deployment
./delete_node_group.sh    # Remove the GPU node group
./delete_cluster.sh       # Delete the EKS cluster&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion-and-next-steps"&gt;Conclusion and next steps&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At this point you have a working inference endpoint, and you got there without maintaining CUDA compatibility yourself, without writing TorchServe handler boilerplate, and without assembling a multi-stage Dockerfile that stitches the GPU stack together. That’s what the Ray Serve DLC is designed to do: provide a supported, pre-tested container so you can focus on model code rather than infrastructure maintenance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For teams currently on TorchServe, this is a strong migration path. The DLC eliminates version drift, simplifies upgrades to a tag swap, and provides regular security patches managed by AWS. For workloads that need to scale beyond a single node, &lt;a href="https://docs.ray.io/en/latest/cluster/kubernetes/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;KubeRay&lt;/a&gt; extends this same foundation to multi-node distributed serving.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, try the accompanying &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-aws-deep-learning-containers/tree/main/inference/ray-serve/ray-serve-single-node" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;code sample&lt;/a&gt; for a complete end-to-end deployment. To browse all available DLC images, including CPU variants and other frameworks, visit the &lt;a href="https://aws.github.io/deep-learning-containers/reference/available_images/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Deep Learning Containers reference&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;About the authors&lt;/h2&gt;

He specializes in architecting distributed Gen AI applications running on NVIDIA GPUs, AWS Trainium and AWS Inferentia using platforms and orchestrators such as Amazon SageMaker HyperPod, Amazon EKS, Ray, SageMaker Serverless Inference, Amazon Nova Forge, and Slurm.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;/div&gt; 
 &lt;div class="blog-author-box" style="padding-top: 2.0em"&gt; 
  &lt;div class="blog-author-image" style="margin-right: 1.0em"&gt;
   &lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignnone size-full" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/08/ML-21647-3.jpg" alt="Jinyan Li" width="100" height="133"&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;

&lt;/footer&gt;</content:encoded>
					
		
		
			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Automate user-level custom permissions for Amazon Quick</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/automate-user-level-custom-permissions-for-amazon-quick/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Ashok Dasineni]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 09 Sep 2026 15:45:24 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Quick Suite]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">a77ad5a4cc8d539af020eba9f19a5636de04f505</guid>

					<description>Amazon Quick custom permissions let you enforce least-privilege access by toggling features per user. This post walks through four patterns to automate custom permissions across the user lifecycle: a RegisterUser API parameter, account and role defaults, event-driven Amazon EventBridge and AWS Lambda automation, and a retroactive batch update script.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;As &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick&lt;/a&gt; environments scale and new AI-powered capabilities expand what users can do, automating user-level custom permissions becomes critical to maintaining the principle of least privilege. To address this, with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/custom-permissions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;custom permissions&lt;/a&gt; in Quick, you can enforce fine-grained access control by toggling specific features on or off for individual users. For example, with custom permissions, you can control access so that financial analysts author reports without exporting raw data, and external partners view dashboards without accessing sharing controls.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;While Quick provides various options to apply custom permissions at the account, role, and user level, there are scenarios where your organization’s specific permissions need to be applied dynamically. This post walks through four architectural patterns to automate custom permissions assignment at key stages of the user lifecycle. The approaches range from a single API parameter to an event-driven automation, covering new users, future users, group-based logic, and retroactive bulk updates.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-we-will-cover"&gt;What we will cover&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We will explore four scenarios to handle key stages of the user lifecycle:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pre-registered users&lt;/strong&gt;: If you build custom portals or scripts, we show how to apply custom permissions proactively at the exact moment of user creation using the &lt;code&gt;RegisterUser&lt;/code&gt; API.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Default account or role permissions:&lt;/strong&gt; To set default custom permissions at the account or role level, we cover how to use the &lt;code&gt;UpdateAccountCustomPermission&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;UpdateRoleCustomPermission&lt;/code&gt; APIs to enforce a default profile for all existing and future users, no additional automation required.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Event-driven custom logic&lt;/strong&gt;: When you need conditional logic beyond native defaults (such as applying different profiles based on group membership) we show how to use Amazon EventBridge and AWS Lambda to automatically detect new group memberships and apply permissions dynamically. This supports both native Quick groups and AWS IAM Identity Center (IDC) groups.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Retroactive batch updates&lt;/strong&gt;: For existing users who were provisioned before automation was in place, we provide a robust Python script to retroactively apply custom permissions to all users in specified Quick groups.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="scenario-1-pre-registered-users-api-and-cli"&gt;Scenario 1: Pre-registered users (API and CLI)&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you have a custom onboarding portal that provisions users using the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quicksight/latest/APIReference/API_RegisterUser.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;RegisterUser&lt;/a&gt; API, you don’t need complex automation. You can apply custom permissions during the creation of the Quick user by including the &lt;code&gt;--custom-permissions-name&lt;/code&gt; parameter in your call.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;When to use this approach&lt;/strong&gt;: Your organization controls the user creation process end-to-end through a custom portal or script. This is the most direct path, no event-driven infrastructure required. This is especially common for software as a service (SaaS) companies embedding Quick across customer accounts. For instance, automatically restricting premium features like paginated reports and GenBI based on a customer’s pricing tier at the moment each user is provisioned.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following example applies a custom permissions profile named &lt;strong&gt;Restricted-Author-Profile&lt;/strong&gt; to Authors in the account, but the same API can be used to apply permissions profiles to any role (including Author Pros, Admins, Admin Pros, Readers, and Reader Pros).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws quicksight register-user \
    --aws-account-id 123456789012 \
    --namespace default \
    --identity-type QUICKSIGHT \
    --user-role AUTHOR \
    --email user@example.com \
    --user-name user_name \
    --custom-permissions-name "Restricted-Author-Profile"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="scenario-2-default-account-or-role-permissions-api-and-cli"&gt;Scenario 2: Default account or role permissions (API and CLI)&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With two native APIs, you can set default custom permission profiles without per-user automation. Note that Quick custom permissions follow a three-level hierarchy (account, role, and user) where user-level settings override role-level, which override account-level, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/business-intelligence/establishing-enterprise-governance-in-amazon-quick-using-custom-permissions/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;allowing administrators to implement flexible, layered security policies&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;When to use this approach:&lt;/strong&gt; These APIs cover both current and future use cases with minimal operational overhead. Start here before building custom automation. Move to Scenario 3 only if you need conditional logic beyond what account or role-level defaults support. For example, a 50,000-user enterprise may need all newly launched GenBI features and connectors blocked by default until their security team completes a 60–90-day review. An account-level default enforces that restriction instantly, without requiring any per-user automation and with no provisioning gap.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="option-a-account-level-default"&gt;Option A: Account-level default&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quicksight/latest/APIReference/API_UpdateAccountCustomPermission.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;UpdateAccountCustomPermission&lt;/a&gt; API sets a fallback custom permission profile that Quick applies to any user who doesn’t have an explicit profile assigned, including new users created with Just-In-Time provisioning.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws quicksight update-account-custom-permission \
    --aws-account-id 123456789012 \
    --custom-permissions-name "Restricted-User-Profile"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="option-b-role-level-default"&gt;Option B: Role-level default&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quicksight/latest/APIReference/API_UpdateRoleCustomPermission.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;UpdateRoleCustomPermission&lt;/a&gt; API, you can set a default custom permission profile per Quick role (READER, AUTHOR, ADMIN, and PRO roles).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws quicksight update-role-custom-permission \
    --aws-account-id 123456789012 \
    --role AUTHOR \
    --namespace default \
    --custom-permissions-name "Restricted-Author-Profile"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="scenario-3-event-driven-custom-logic-amazon-eventbridge-and-lambda"&gt;Scenario 3: Event-driven custom logic (Amazon EventBridge and Lambda)&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This scenario addresses more granular requirements: applying different custom permissions profiles to users based on which Quick or IAM Identity Center group they belong to. For example, a 125,000-employee technology services company needs authors in each business unit to receive distinct permission profiles at the moment of group assignment. This prevents cross-unit asset sharing while granting power-user access only to approved individuals, even though all authors share the same Quick role. Because we don’t have a native API for assigning custom permissions to a Group, we will need an architecture that detects when a user is added to a group that should have specific permissions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note: We suggest combining this approach with Scenario 2 for a fully layered permissions strategy.&lt;/strong&gt; Scenarios 2 and 3 are complementary, not alternatives. When a user is provisioned through Just-In-Time federation, there’s an unavoidable window between account creation and the moment an administrator adds them to the appropriate group. Scenario 3 only fires on the group membership event.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To keep users from ever being in an unrestricted state, apply Scenario 2 first as a baseline: set an account-level or role-level default that enforces your most restrictive acceptable profile. Then use Scenario 3 to refine permissions once the user is assigned to a group. The user-level override from Scenario 3 will take precedence over the Scenario 2 default, so there is no conflict, only complementary layers of control.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Consider a global bank with more than 200,000 users, provisioned through single sign-on (SSO), that must block data export the instant an employee joins. Scenario 2 closes that gap immediately with a restrictive account-level default, and Scenario 3 refines permissions once the user is assigned to their compliance group. This prevents employees from downloading sensitive client data during the window between provisioning and group assignment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Quick and IAM Identity Center emit distinct AWS CloudTrail events for group membership changes. Quick emits &lt;code&gt;CreateGroupMembership&lt;/code&gt; when a user is added and &lt;code&gt;DeleteGroupMembership&lt;/code&gt; when removed. IAM Identity Center emits &lt;code&gt;AddMemberToGroup&lt;/code&gt; when a user is added and &lt;code&gt;RemoveMemberFromGroup&lt;/code&gt; when removed. The following architecture detects these events to trigger permission updates automatically.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We will build this using Amazon EventBridge (to detect the event) and AWS Lambda (to apply the fix).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 541px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/02/ML-21102-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/02/ML-21102-1.png" alt="Event-driven flow where a group membership change captured by CloudTrail triggers an Amazon EventBridge rule and a Lambda function that applies a Quick custom permission profile" width="531"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Event-driven architecture that applies a custom permission profile when a user is added to a Quick or IAM Identity Center group&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A user is added to a Quick or Identity Center group.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon CloudTrail:&lt;/strong&gt; Captures the &lt;code&gt;CreateGroupMembership&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;AddMemberToGroup&lt;/code&gt; events.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon EventBridge:&lt;/strong&gt; Filters these logs to identify when a user is successfully added to the group.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Lambda:&lt;/strong&gt; Extracts the user details and applies the correct permission profile using the &lt;code&gt;UpdateUserCustomPermission&lt;/code&gt; API.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before implementing this solution, confirm you have the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An &lt;a href="https://signin.aws.amazon.com/signin?redirect_uri=https%3A%2F%2Fportal.aws.amazon.com%2Fbilling%2Fsignup%2Fresume&amp;amp;client_id=signup" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS account&lt;/a&gt; with administrative access.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)&lt;/a&gt; permissions to create and manage AWS resources using AWS CloudFormation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Access to the following AWS services: 
  &lt;ol type="a"&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://aws.amazon.com/cloudformation" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CloudFormation&lt;/a&gt;: deploys and manages the infrastructure stack as code.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick&lt;/a&gt;: the target service where custom permissions are applied.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudtrail/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CloudTrail&lt;/a&gt;: must be enabled in the target AWS Region. Captures Quick and IDC API events that Amazon EventBridge consumes.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eventbridge/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon EventBridge&lt;/a&gt;: filters CloudTrail events to detect group membership changes.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/lambda/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Lambda&lt;/a&gt;: executes the automation logic that calls the &lt;code&gt;UpdateUserCustomPermission&lt;/code&gt; API.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS IAM Identity Center&lt;/a&gt;: required only if using IDC group-based triggers.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ol&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Python 3.9+ and AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI) v2 are required for running the batch update script in Scenario 4.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="deploy-with-cloudformation"&gt;Deploy with CloudFormation&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can deploy the full pipeline (IAM role, Lambda function, and Amazon EventBridge rule) using the provided CloudFormation template. The CloudFormation stack must be deployed in the same AWS Region as your Amazon Quick subscription, since Amazon EventBridge rules only capture events within their own Region.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://us-east-1.console.aws.amazon.com/cloudformation/home?region=us-east-1#/stacks/create/template?stackName=quickcustompermissions&amp;amp;templateURL=https://aws-blogs-artifacts-public.s3.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/artifacts/ML-21102/AutomateUser-LevelCustomPermissionsforAmazonQuick.yaml" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignnone wp-image-75141 size-full" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2024/04/29/launch-stack-button.png" alt="" width="190" height="36"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The template accepts the following parameters:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Parameter&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Description&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;UseIdentityCenter&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Set to true if your Quick account uses IAM Identity Center&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;TargetGroupName&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt; &lt;p&gt;The name of the group to apply custom permissions to. For Quick groups, this is the Quick group name. For IDC groups, this is the IDC group DisplayName.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;Important: Each deployment targets either a Quick group or an IDC group, never both. To monitor both group types, deploy separate stacks.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;PermissionProfileName&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Name of the Quick custom permissions profile to apply to members of this group. This profile must already exist in your Quick account before deployment.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;QuickNamespace&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Quick namespace for user management.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;LambdaFunctionPrefixName&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Prefix used for the Lambda function name.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you prefer to deploy manually, follow these steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-1-create-the-iam-role-for-lambda"&gt;Step 1: Create the IAM role for Lambda&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Your Lambda function needs permission to interact with Quick.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Go to the &lt;strong&gt;IAM Console&lt;/strong&gt; → &lt;strong&gt;Policies&lt;/strong&gt; → &lt;strong&gt;Create policy&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Switch to the &lt;strong&gt;JSON&lt;/strong&gt; tab and paste this policy: 
  &lt;ol type="a"&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note&lt;/strong&gt;: the permissions to IdentityStore are only needed if your Quick account is integrated with IAM Identity Center.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ol&gt; 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Statement": [
        {
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": [
                "logs:CreateLogGroup",
                "logs:CreateLogStream",
                "logs:PutLogEvents"
            ],
            "Resource": "arn:aws:logs:*:YOUR_ACCOUNT_ID:log-group:/aws/lambda/Auto-Assign-QS-Permissions:*"
        },
        {
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": [
                "quicksight:UpdateUserCustomPermission",
                "quicksight:DeleteUserCustomPermission",
                "quicksight:UpdateUser",
                "quicksight:DescribeUser"
            ],
            "Resource": "arn:aws:quicksight:*:*:user/*"
        },
        {
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": [
                "identitystore:DescribeUser",
                "identitystore:DescribeGroup"
            ],
            "Resource": "*"
        }
    ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Name the policy &lt;code&gt;Quick-Lambda-Policy&lt;/code&gt; and choose &lt;strong&gt;Create policy&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Navigate to &lt;strong&gt;Roles&lt;/strong&gt; → &lt;strong&gt;Create role&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select &lt;strong&gt;AWS Service&lt;/strong&gt; and choose &lt;strong&gt;Lambda&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Next&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Search for and select the &lt;code&gt;Quick-Lambda-Policy&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Next&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Name the Role &lt;code&gt;Quick-Auto-Permissions-Role&lt;/code&gt; and select &lt;strong&gt;Create role&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-2-deploy-the-lambda-function"&gt;Step 2: Deploy the Lambda function&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Go to the &lt;strong&gt;Lambda Console&lt;/strong&gt; → &lt;strong&gt;Create function&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Function name:&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;code&gt;Auto-Assign-QS-Permissions&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Runtime:&lt;/strong&gt; Python 3.14 (or latest available).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Execution role:&lt;/strong&gt; Use another role → Select &lt;code&gt;Quick-Auto-Permissions-Role&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Create function&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Under &lt;strong&gt;Configuration&lt;/strong&gt; → &lt;strong&gt;General configuration&lt;/strong&gt;, increase the timeout to 30 seconds. 
  &lt;ol type="a"&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;This should be sufficient for single-event processing. If you observe timeouts in Amazon CloudWatch Logs, increase this value accordingly.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ol&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Set the following environment variables under &lt;strong&gt;Configuration&lt;/strong&gt; → &lt;strong&gt;Environment Variables:&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Variable Name&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Description&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;PERMISSION_PROFILE&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Exact name of the custom permissions profile created in Quick to apply&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;TARGET_GROUP_NAME&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Name of the group to monitor (Quick group name or IDC group DisplayName)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;NAMESPACE&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Quick namespace (typically “default”)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;ol start="8" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Navigate back to the &lt;strong&gt;Code Source&lt;/strong&gt; editor and paste the following code: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import logging
import os
import time
import boto3

logger = logging.getLogger()
logger.setLevel(logging.INFO)

quicksight = boto3.client("quicksight")
identity_store = boto3.client("identitystore")

def lambda_handler(event, context):
    detail = event.get("detail", {})
    event_source = detail.get("eventSource", "")
    event_name = detail.get("eventName", "")
    permission_profile = os.environ["PERMISSION_PROFILE"]
    target_group = os.environ["TARGET_GROUP_NAME"]
    namespace = os.environ["NAMESPACE"]

    account_id = (
        detail.get("userIdentity", {}).get("accountId")
        or detail.get("recipientAccountId")
    )

    logger.info(
        "Event received: source=%s, name=%s", event_source, event_name
    )

    try:
        if event_source == "sso-directory.amazonaws.com":
            return handle_idc_event(detail, account_id, namespace, permission_profile, target_group)
        elif event_source == "quicksight.amazonaws.com":
            return handle_quicksight_event(detail, account_id, namespace, permission_profile, target_group)
        else:
            logger.warning("Unknown event source: %s", event_source)
            return {"statusCode": 200, "body": "Unknown event source"}
    except Exception as e:
        logger.error("Error processing event: %s", str(e))
        return {"statusCode": 500, "body": str(e)}

def handle_idc_event(detail, account_id, namespace, permission_profile, target_group):
    """Handle AddMemberToGroup or RemoveMemberFromGroup from sso-directory.amazonaws.com."""
    request_params = detail.get("requestParameters") or {}
    identity_store_id = request_params.get("identityStoreId")
    group_id = request_params.get("groupId")
    # AddMemberToGroup nests memberId under "member"; RemoveMemberFromGroup puts it at top level
    member_id = (request_params.get("memberId")
        or (request_params.get("member") or {}).get("memberId"))

    if not identity_store_id or not group_id or not member_id:
        logger.warning("Missing identityStoreId, groupId, or memberId.")
        return {"statusCode": 200, "body": "Missing IDC event info"}

    # Resolve group name from group ID
    group_resp = identity_store.describe_group(
        IdentityStoreId=identity_store_id,
        GroupId=group_id,
    )
    group_name = group_resp.get("DisplayName", "")
    logger.info("IDC group resolved: '%s' (ID: %s)", group_name, group_id)

    if group_name != target_group:
        logger.info(
            "Group '%s' does not match target '%s'. Skipping.",
            group_name,
            target_group,
        )
        return {"statusCode": 200, "body": f"Group {group_name} not targeted"}

    # Resolve user name from member ID
    idc_user = identity_store.describe_user(
        IdentityStoreId=identity_store_id,
        UserId=member_id,
    )
    user_name = idc_user.get("UserName")
    if not user_name:
        logger.warning("No UserName found for IDC user %s", member_id)
        return {"statusCode": 200, "body": "No UserName in IDC"}

    logger.info("IDC user resolved: %s (ID: %s)", user_name, member_id)

    # Wait for Quick to sync the user
    qs_user = None
    for attempt in range(5):
        try:
            qs_user = quicksight.describe_user(
                AwsAccountId=account_id,
                Namespace=namespace,
                UserName=user_name,
            )
            break
        except quicksight.exceptions.ResourceNotFoundException:
            logger.info(
                "User '%s' not yet in Quick (attempt %d/5). Waiting...",
                user_name,
                attempt + 1,
            )
            time.sleep(3)

    if qs_user is None:
        logger.warning(
            "User '%s' not found in Quick after retries.",
            user_name,
        )
        return {"statusCode": 200, "body": f"User {user_name} not in Quick"}

    event_name = detail.get("eventName", "")
    if event_name == "RemoveMemberFromGroup":
        return remove_permissions(account_id, namespace, user_name)
    else:
        return apply_permissions(account_id, namespace, user_name, permission_profile)

def handle_quicksight_event(detail, account_id, namespace, permission_profile, target_group):
    """Handle CreateGroupMembership or DeleteGroupMembership from quicksight.amazonaws.com."""
    request_params = detail.get("requestParameters") or {}
    group_name = request_params.get("groupName", "")
    member_name = request_params.get("memberName", "")

    if not group_name or not member_name:
        logger.warning("Missing groupName or memberName in event.")
        return {"statusCode": 200, "body": "Missing event info"}

    event_name = detail.get("eventName", "")
    action = "removed from" if event_name == "DeleteGroupMembership" else "added to"
    logger.info(
        "Quick group membership: user '%s' %s '%s'",
        member_name,
        action,
        group_name,
    )

    if group_name != target_group:
        logger.info(
            "Group '%s' does not match target '%s'. Skipping.",
            group_name,
            target_group,
        )
        return {"statusCode": 200, "body": f"Group {group_name} not targeted"}

    if event_name == "DeleteGroupMembership":
        return remove_permissions(account_id, namespace, member_name)
    else:
        return apply_permissions(account_id, namespace, member_name, permission_profile)

def apply_permissions(account_id, namespace, user_name, permission_profile):
    """Apply the custom permission profile to a Quick user."""
    quicksight.update_user_custom_permission(
        AwsAccountId=account_id,
        Namespace=namespace,
        UserName=user_name,
        CustomPermissionsName=permission_profile,
    )
    logger.info(
        "Permission profile '%s' applied to user '%s'.",
        permission_profile,
        user_name,
    )
    return {
        "statusCode": 200,
        "body": f"Permission applied for user: {user_name}",
    }

def remove_permissions(account_id, namespace, user_name):
    """Remove custom permissions, reverting user to account/role-level default."""
    try:
        quicksight.delete_user_custom_permission(
            AwsAccountId=account_id,
            Namespace=namespace,
            UserName=user_name,
        )
        logger.info("Permission removed for user '%s'.", user_name)
        return {
            "statusCode": 200,
            "body": f"Permission removed for user: {user_name}",
        }
    except quicksight.exceptions.ResourceNotFoundException:
        logger.info("User '%s' had no custom permission to remove.", user_name)
        return {"statusCode": 200, "body": f"No permission to remove for: {user_name}"}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Deploy&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-3-create-the-amazon-eventbridge-rule"&gt;Step 3: Create the Amazon EventBridge rule&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Now we must configure this function so that it runs only when a user is successfully added to a group.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Go to the &lt;strong&gt;Amazon EventBridge Console&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select &lt;strong&gt;Rules&lt;/strong&gt; → &lt;strong&gt;Create rule&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Opt out of the &lt;strong&gt;Visual rule builder&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Under &lt;strong&gt;Name&lt;/strong&gt; enter &lt;code&gt;Quick-Group-Membership-Creation-Rule&lt;/code&gt; and choose &lt;strong&gt;Next&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select &lt;strong&gt;Other&lt;/strong&gt; under &lt;strong&gt;Event source&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Paste the following into the JSON editor for &lt;strong&gt;Event pattern:&lt;/strong&gt;.When the Quick account is integrated with IDC, the source of this event will be the IDC identity store whereas the source will be Quick otherwise. Therefore, the Amazon EventBridge rule we will create will depend on the account authentication mechanism:&lt;strong&gt;Option A: IAM Identity Center&lt;/strong&gt; 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "source": ["aws.sso-directory"],
    "detail-type": ["AWS API Call via CloudTrail"],
    "detail": {
        "eventSource": ["sso-directory.amazonaws.com"],
        "eventName": ["AddMemberToGroup", "RemoveMemberFromGroup"],
        "errorCode": [{ "exists": false }]
    }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Option B: Other Authentication (using Quick Groups)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "source": ["aws.quicksight"],
    "detail-type": ["AWS API Call via CloudTrail"],
    "detail": {
        "eventSource": ["quicksight.amazonaws.com"],
        "eventName": ["CreateGroupMembership", "DeleteGroupMembership"],
        "errorCode": [{ "exists": false }]
    }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select &lt;strong&gt;AWS Service&lt;/strong&gt; as the &lt;strong&gt;Target type&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select &lt;strong&gt;Lambda function&lt;/strong&gt; as the target and choose &lt;code&gt;Auto-Assign-QS-Permissions&lt;/code&gt; (the function you built in Step 2).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Next&lt;/strong&gt; and &lt;strong&gt;Create rule&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-4-add-the-amazon-eventbridge-trigger"&gt;Step 4: Add the Amazon EventBridge trigger&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Return to the &lt;code&gt;Auto-Assign-QS-Permissions&lt;/code&gt; Lambda function.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Navigate to &lt;strong&gt;Configuration&lt;/strong&gt; → &lt;strong&gt;Triggers → Add trigger&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select &lt;strong&gt;Amazon EventBridge&lt;/strong&gt; as the source.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Existing rules&lt;/strong&gt; and select the &lt;code&gt;Quick-Group-Membership-Creation-Rule&lt;/code&gt; created in Step 3.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Add&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-5-verify-the-setup"&gt;Step 5: Verify the setup&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To test this, add a user to a Group in Quick or IDC:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Test:&lt;/strong&gt; Manually add a new or existing user to the target Group.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Monitor:&lt;/strong&gt; Go to the CloudWatch Logs for your Lambda function.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Confirm:&lt;/strong&gt; You should see a log entry like: Permission profile ‘123’ applied to user ‘XYZ’.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Result:&lt;/strong&gt; When that user logs in, they will be restricted by the profile.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="design-considerations"&gt;Design considerations&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Group removal:&lt;/strong&gt; This solution for Scenario 3 already handles removal events. When a user is removed from the target group, the Lambda calls &lt;code&gt;DeleteUserCustomPermission&lt;/code&gt;, reverting them to the account or role-level default from Scenario 2. No additional configuration is needed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Multi-group membership:&lt;/strong&gt; Quick supports only one custom permissions profile per user at a time. If your organization assigns users to multiple groups with different profiles, you can extend the Lambda logic to list all groups the user belongs to, look up the profile mapped to each group, and apply the highest-priority profile (for example, the most restrictive profile). This multi-group conflict resolution isn’t included in the provided solution and must be implemented by your team based on your organization’s specific priority rules.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="scenario-4-batch-update-for-existing-group-members"&gt;Scenario 4: Batch update for existing group members&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You likely have existing groups (for example, “Finance-Readers” or “Marketing-Authors”) containing users who need specific restrictions applied retroactively. Because these users were created in the past, the Amazon EventBridge automation (Scenario 3) won’t catch them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To fix this, we use a Python script to iterate through a specific &lt;strong&gt;Quick Group&lt;/strong&gt; and apply the custom permission profile to every member.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-challenge-pagination"&gt;The challenge: Pagination&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Quick groups can contain thousands of users. The API &lt;code&gt;ListGroupMemberships&lt;/code&gt; only returns &lt;strong&gt;100 members at a time&lt;/strong&gt;. If you write a script without pagination logic, it will stop after the first 100 users, leaving the rest unsecured. The following script handles this automatically using &lt;code&gt;NextToken&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Copy the following code. You only need to edit the &lt;strong&gt;Configuration Section&lt;/strong&gt; at the top.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import boto3
import sys
import time
import csv
from botocore.exceptions import ClientError

# =========================================================
# CONFIGURATION - Edit these values before running
# =========================================================

# 1. Your AWS Account ID
AWS_ACCOUNT_ID = '279938032093'

# 2. The Quick Group whose members you want to target
# (e.g., 'finance-users', 'marketing-group')
TARGET_GROUP_NAME = 'BlogTestGroup'

# 3. The name of the Custom Permission Profile you want to apply
# (Must already exist in Quick)
PERMISSION_PROFILE_NAME = 'TestBlog'

# 4. Namespace (almost always 'default')
NAMESPACE = 'default'

# =========================================================

# Initialize the Quick client
qs = boto3.client('quicksight')

def get_all_group_members(group_name):
    """
    Generator function that yields members one by one,
    handling pagination automatically.
    """
    print(f"Fetching members from group: '{group_name}'...")
    next_token = None
    while True:
        try:
            # Construct the API call arguments
            api_args = {
                'AwsAccountId': AWS_ACCOUNT_ID,
                'Namespace': NAMESPACE,
                'GroupName': group_name,
                'MaxResults': 100
            }
            if next_token:
                api_args['NextToken'] = next_token

            # Call API
            response = qs.list_group_memberships(**api_args)

            # Yield members from the current page
            for member in response.get('GroupMemberList', []):
                # We only need the MemberName (which corresponds to UserName)
                yield member.get('MemberName')

            # Check if there are more pages
            next_token = response.get('NextToken')
            if not next_token:
                break

        except qs.exceptions.ResourceNotFoundException:
            print(f"ERROR: The group '{group_name}' does not exist.")
            sys.exit(1)
        except ClientError as e:
            print(f"CRITICAL API ERROR: {e}")
            sys.exit(1)

def apply_permission(user_name):
    """
    Applies the custom permission profile to a single user.
    """
    try:
        qs.update_user_custom_permission(
            AwsAccountId=AWS_ACCOUNT_ID,
            UserName=user_name,
            Namespace=NAMESPACE,
            CustomPermissionsName=PERMISSION_PROFILE_NAME
        )
        return True, "Success"
    except qs.exceptions.ResourceNotFoundException as e:
        error_msg = str(e)
        if 'User' in error_msg or 'user' in error_msg:
            return False, "User not found (might have been deleted)"
        else:
            return False, f"Profile '{PERMISSION_PROFILE_NAME}' not found"
    except ClientError as e:
        return False, str(e)

def main():
    print("--------------------------------------------")
    print(f"STARTING BATCH UPDATE")
    print(f"Target Group: {TARGET_GROUP_NAME}")
    print(f"Applying Profile: {PERMISSION_PROFILE_NAME}")
    print("--------------------------------------------\n")

    success_count = 0
    fail_count = 0
    failed_users = []

    # Iterate through the generator
    for user_name in get_all_group_members(TARGET_GROUP_NAME):
        print(f"Processing: {user_name}...", end=" ")
        status, message = apply_permission(user_name)

        if status:
            print("OK")
            success_count += 1
        else:
            print(f"FAILED ({message})")
            fail_count += 1
            failed_users.append({'UserName': user_name, 'Error': message})

        # Rate Limiting: Sleep briefly to avoid hitting API limits
        time.sleep(0.1)

    print("\n--------------------------------------------")
    print("JOB COMPLETED")
    print(f"Total Success: {success_count}")
    print(f"Total Failed: {fail_count}")
    print("--------------------------------------------")

    # Export failed users to CSV for review/retry
    if failed_users:
        csv_filename = f"failed_users_{TARGET_GROUP_NAME}.csv"
        with open(csv_filename, 'w', newline='') as f:
            writer = csv.DictWriter(f, fieldnames=['UserName', 'Error'])
            writer.writeheader()
            writer.writerows(failed_users)
        print(f"\nFailed users written to: {csv_filename}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; The default delay of 0.1 seconds between API calls (~10 requests/second) is suitable for most deployments. For large-scale groups (over 10,000 members), increase &lt;code&gt;API_DELAY_SECONDS&lt;/code&gt; to avoid API throttling. Monitor for ThrottlingException errors in the output and adjust as needed for your environment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="detailed-execution-steps"&gt;Detailed execution steps&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Prepare your environment&lt;/strong&gt;.You can run this locally or in &lt;strong&gt;AWS CloudShell&lt;/strong&gt; directly in your browser, which comes pre-authenticated and has Boto3 installed. 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Sign in to the AWS Management Console.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Choose the &lt;strong&gt;CloudShell&lt;/strong&gt; icon (terminal icon) in the top navigation bar.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Create the script file&lt;/strong&gt;.In the CloudShell terminal, enter the following command to open a text editor:&lt;code&gt;nano update_permissions.py&lt;/code&gt; 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Paste the preceding Python code into the editor.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Edit the &lt;code&gt;AWS_ACCOUNT_ID&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;TARGET_GROUP_NAME&lt;/code&gt; variables at the top of the file to match your environment.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Press Ctrl+X, then Y, then Enter to save and exit.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Run the script&lt;/strong&gt;.Execute the script with Python:&lt;code&gt;python3 update_permissions.py&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Verify results&lt;/strong&gt;. 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;The script will print “OK” next to every user it successfully updates.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;If any users fail, the script writes a &lt;code&gt;failed_users_{GROUP_NAME}.csv&lt;/code&gt; file with the username and failure reason for each. Review this file to identify users that need manual follow-up.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;If you see “Profile not found,” double-check that you created the Custom Permission Profile in the Amazon Quick console exactly as spelled in the script.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Run &lt;code&gt;aws quicksight describe-user --aws-account-id &amp;lt;ACCOUNT_ID&amp;gt; --namespace &amp;lt;NAMESPACE&amp;gt; --user-name &amp;lt;USERNAME&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt; and confirm that the &lt;code&gt;CustomPermissionsName&lt;/code&gt; field in the response matches your expected profile.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you deployed using the CloudFormation template, delete the stack to remove all resources it created, including the Amazon EventBridge rule, Lambda function, and associated IAM role and policy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you deployed manually, delete the following resources:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The Amazon EventBridge rule (&lt;code&gt;Quick-Group-Membership-Creation-Rule&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The Lambda function (&lt;code&gt;Auto-Assign-QS-Permissions&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The IAM role (&lt;code&gt;Quick-Auto-Permissions-Role&lt;/code&gt;) and policy (&lt;code&gt;Quick-Lambda-Policy&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The batch update script (Scenario 4) does not deploy any infrastructure and requires no clean up.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Automating custom permissions in Amazon Quick is a foundational step toward a secure, scalable user governance strategy. As your Quick environment grows, the patterns in this post help verify that authentication and authorization scale together, not independently.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following table summarizes the four scenarios and when to apply each:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scenario&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Trigger&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Best For&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1. Pre-registered users&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;RegisterUser&lt;/code&gt; API call&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Organizations with custom onboarding portals&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2. Account/role defaults&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Native API, always on&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Consistent restrictions across all users or by role&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3. Event-driven group logic&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Group membership added&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Custom permissions logic like profiles per group; IDC or Quick groups&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;4. Retroactive batch&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Manual script execution&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Existing users provisioned before automation was in place&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One manual step remains in the Scenario 3 flow: an administrator must add each user to the appropriate group before the event-driven pipeline fires. You can remove this step entirely by extending the same Amazon EventBridge + Lambda architecture to listen for new user creation events.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When new users are provisioned through Just-In-Time federation, CloudTrail captures a &lt;code&gt;CreateUser&lt;/code&gt; event (when Quick users are invited by admins, this produces a &lt;code&gt;BatchCreateUser&lt;/code&gt; event). A Lambda function can intercept that event and apply custom logic to determine the correct group based on email domain, internal user mapping, or any available user attribute. It then calls &lt;code&gt;CreateGroupMembership&lt;/code&gt; to assign the user to that group automatically.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Combined with Scenario 3, this approach can create a fully automated pipeline with no manual steps. A full implementation of this extension is beyond the scope of this post. However, the building blocks (CloudTrail event capture, Amazon EventBridge filtering, and Lambda-based Quick API calls) are identical to those described in Scenario 3.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Start with Scenario 2 to establish your baseline today. For more details, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/custom-permissions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick Custom Permissions&lt;/a&gt; in the Amazon Quick User Guide.&lt;/p&gt;

  &lt;p style="overflow: hidden"&gt;Ashok is a Solutions Architect for Amazon Quick Suite. Before joining AWS, Ashok worked with clients and organizations in the banking and financial domain, focusing on fraud research and prevention. He designed and implemented innovative solutions to improve business process, reduce cost, and increase revenue, helping companies around the world achieve their highest potential through data.&lt;/p&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Take on your most ambitious work with GPT-6 Astra on Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/take-on-your-most-ambitious-work-with-gpt-6-astra-on-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Tanvi Girinath]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 08 Sep 2026 22:06:58 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">68d48019de714857964056351fc4d79e51c3ab9f</guid>

					<description>GPT-6 Astra from OpenAI is now generally available on Amazon Bedrock. It brings deeper reasoning and sharper judgment to your most demanding tasks, running on the Amazon Bedrock inference engine built for high performance, security, and scale.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;GPT-6 Astra from OpenAI brings greater depth and judgment to your most demanding tasks and runs on the Amazon Bedrock inference engine built for high performance, security, and scale.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Organizations are already running AI agents that write code, analyze data, and automate complex workflows at production scale on Amazon Bedrock. GPT-6 Astra raises the potential of what those agents can deliver. It applies deeper reasoning and sharper judgment to complex business decisions, works across software and files, and produces professional-quality output aligned with organizational voice, templates, and standards.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Today, GPT-6 Astra, the latest and most capable OpenAI model, is generally available on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;. You can call the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/openai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;model directly&lt;/a&gt; through the Amazon Bedrock APIs or configure ChatGPT Work and Codex to use GPT-6 Astra on Amazon Bedrock. As part of this launch, OpenAI is also introducing new enterprise plugins for ChatGPT Work that extend Astra’s browser-use capabilities across common business applications. The Amazon Bedrock inference engine delivers the scalability and reliability required for production. Established AWS controls help you secure workloads, govern access, and audit model invocation activity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="greater-depth-for-complex-decisions"&gt;Greater depth for complex decisions&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GPT-6 Astra brings greater depth to work that requires you to reconcile competing inputs, trace dependencies, and determine what to prioritize. When performing financial analysis, it can help you reconcile conflicting data sources and identify discrepancies that could change a recommendation. During contract review, it can review hundreds of pages within a context window of up to 1 million input tokens and identify provisions that carry the greatest risk. Across a codebase, it can investigate problems across a large codebase, reason through dependencies, and carry a fix from diagnosis through testing. GPT-6 Astra also advances computer and browser use, allowing it to work across applications and continue workflows directly through software interfaces when an API or connector is unavailable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For workflows that reuse the same context across requests, such as recurring document review, codebase analysis, or agents grounded in company standards, GPT-6 Astra supports both implicit and explicit prompt caching. With explicit caching, you can set cache breakpoints to control which context is cached. Reusing that context in subsequent requests helps reduce repeated processing, cost, and latency.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="layered-security-and-governance-for-production-ai"&gt;Layered security and governance for production AI&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Model-level safeguards work alongside the security and governance controls of Amazon Bedrock. OpenAI evaluated GPT-6 Astra through its &lt;a href="https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/18a02b5d-6b67-4cec-ab64-68cdfbddebcd/preparedness-framework-v2.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Preparedness Framework&lt;/a&gt;, which assesses model capabilities across safety-relevant domains and applies progressively stronger safeguards as those capabilities advance. It’s the first OpenAI model to reach the Critical classification for cybersecurity capability. At this level, automated safeguards monitor misuse in real time and can pause or stop activity that exceeds defined boundaries, and work within the Amazon Bedrock service boundary. These safeguards complement AWS security controls when GPT-6 Astra works across code, software, and tools.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock protects your inference data and governs access at every model invocation. Zero-operator access is enforced at the chip, so even AWS operators can’t access your prompts and completions during inference. Data is encrypted in transit and at rest. Access is governed by your AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) policies, and every invocation is logged in AWS CloudTrail. You can route traffic through virtual private cloud (VPC) endpoints using AWS PrivateLink and establish data perimeter policies at the organization level to help prevent data exfiltration across account and network boundaries.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Your inference data isn’t used for model training, and using GPT-6 Astra doesn’t require you to opt into sharing your data with OpenAI. For &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/abuse-detection.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;automated abuse detection&lt;/a&gt;, classifier-flagged traffic is retained by AWS for up to 30 days and processed programmatically. You can request zero data retention through your AWS account team. See &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/data-retention.html#data-retention-zdr" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;data retention&lt;/a&gt; for details.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="build-code-and-work-with-astra"&gt;Build, code, and work with Astra&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can use&amp;nbsp;GPT-6 Astra for inference, knowledge work, and software development.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="for-platform-and-application-teams"&gt;Integrate Astra into your applications&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can integrate GPT-6 Astra directly into your applications using supported Amazon Bedrock APIs. Use it to power autonomous agents that handle complex, multistep workflows, build internal tools that analyze and synthesize documents at scale, or create customer-facing applications that require judgment across competing inputs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="for-business-and-operations-teams"&gt;Turn complex tasks into finished deliverables&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;ChatGPT Work is a productivity agent for turning complex business tasks into finished deliverables. With GPT-6 Astra, it can gather information across applications and files, use the web, and produce spreadsheets, slides, documents, and sites. You can control which applications and websites the agent can access, manage file uploads and downloads, and require confirmation before specific actions. You can also follow its progress, change direction, and approve important steps, keeping people in control throughout the workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;ChatGPT Work is available through the ChatGPT desktop app for Mac and Windows. New enterprise plugins introduced alongside this release extend browser-use capabilities across business intelligence tools, Workday, Navan, and Avalara for tasks across data analytics, operations, and finance. These plugins work through your existing user account and within the permissions your administrators establish, so they do not grant Astra additional access.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="for-development-teams"&gt;Build, test, and ship code faster&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Codex is a software engineering agent that works with local files, repositories, terminals, developer tools, and development environments to write features, fix bugs, run tests, and open pull requests. When you configure Codex to use GPT-6 Astra on Amazon Bedrock, it applies Astra’s reasoning and computer-use capabilities to tasks spanning investigation, implementation, and testing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can access Codex through the ChatGPT desktop app, CLI, VS Code, JetBrains IDEs, and Xcode. For AWS development, the&amp;nbsp;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/agent-toolkit/latest/userguide/quick-start.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Agent Toolkit for AWS&lt;/a&gt;&amp;nbsp;connects&amp;nbsp;Codex to AWS documentation, APIs, and service capabilities with a single terminal command, so your agent can help you develop, deploy, and manage applications more efficiently.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="get-started"&gt;Get started&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can get started in the &lt;a href="https://us-east-1.console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/home?region=us-east-1#/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock console&lt;/a&gt; or programmatically through supported Amazon Bedrock APIs. For information about supported &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-region-compatibility.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Regions&lt;/a&gt;, endpoints, APIs, features, inference profiles and pricing, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-cards-openai.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Interested in how Amazon Bedrock can support your team? &lt;a href="https://pages.awscloud.com/Amazon-Bedrock-Contact-Us.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Connect with us&lt;/a&gt; to start the conversation.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
